Katalog Berita (Halaman 2/57)

#11141. “Giant superatoms” could finally solve quantum computing’s biggest problem

Researchers at Chalmers University of Technology in Sweden have introduced a new theoretical design for quantum systems based on what they call "giant superatoms." This concept offers a fresh way to protect, control, and share quantum information, potentially bringing scientists closer to building large-scale quantum computers.

Quantum computers are expected to transform fields like drug discovery and encryption by solving problems that are far beyond the reach of conventional machines. However, progress has been limited by a major challenge known as decoherence. This occurs when quantum bits, or qubits, lose their information due to interactions with their surroundings. Even small amounts of electromagnetic noise can disrupt the fragile quantum states needed for computation.

"Quantum systems are extraordinarily powerful but also extremely fragile. The key to making them useful is learning how to control their interaction with the surrounding environment," says Lei Du, postdoctoral researcher in applied quantum technology at Chalmers.

Lei Du is the lead author of a study that outlines this new type of quantum system. The design is built around giant superatoms, which combine several important features. These systems reduce decoherence, remain stable, and consist of multiple interconnected "atoms" that function together as a single unit.

Giant superatoms bring together two previously separate ideas in quantum physics: giant atoms and superatoms. While each has been studied on its own, this is the first time they have been merged into a single system. These structures behave like atoms but are not found in nature. Instead, they are engineered by scientists (see fact box below).

The idea of giant atoms was first introduced by researchers at Chalmers over a decade ago and is now widely used in the field. A giant atom is typically designed as a qubit (which is the smallest unit of quantum information). Unlike ordinary atoms, it connects to light or sound waves at multiple, physically separated points. This allows it to interact with its environment in several places at once, helping it preserve quantum information.

"Waves that leave one connection point can travel through the environment and return to affect the atom at another point -- similar to hearing an echo of your own voice before you've finished speaking. This self-interaction leads to highly beneficial quantum effects, reduces decoherence and gives the system a form of memory of past interactions," explains Anton Frisk Kockum, Associate Professor of Applied Quantum Physics at Chalmers and co-author of the study.

Although giant atoms have improved understanding of quantum behavior, they have had limitations when it comes to entanglement. Entanglement allows multiple qubits to share a single quantum state and act as one coordinated system, which is essential for powerful quantum computers.

To overcome this limitation, the research team combined giant atoms with the concept of superatoms. A superatom consists of several natural atoms that share the same quantum state and behave collectively as one larger atom.

This combination is expected to make it easier to create complex quantum states needed for quantum communication, networks, and highly sensitive measurement systems.

"A giant superatom may be envisaged as multiple giant atoms working together as a single entity, exhibiting a non-local interaction between light and matter. This enables quantum information from multiple qubits to be stored and controlled within one unit, without the need for increasingly complex surrounding circuitry," explains Lei Du.

"Giant superatoms open the door to entirely new capabilities, giving us a powerful new toolbox. They allow us to control quantum information and create entanglement in ways that were previously extremely difficult, or even impossible," says Janine Splettstoesser, Professor of Applied Quantum Physics at Chalmers and co-author of the study.

Toward Scalable and Practical Quantum Systems

This work creates new possibilities for building quantum systems that are both scalable and reliable. The researchers plan to move from theory toward actually constructing these systems. Their design could also be integrated with other quantum technologies, serving as a building block for connecting different types of quantum platforms.

"There is currently strong interest in hybrid approaches, in which different quantum systems work together, because each has its own strengths," says Anton Frisk Kockum. "Our research shows that smart design can reduce the need for increasingly complex hardware and giant superatoms are bringing us one step closer to practically applicable quantum technology."

More on: Methods for protecting, controlling and distributing quantum information

The study shows that the way giant superatoms interact with light depends on their internal quantum states. This discovery gives researchers greater control over how quantum information moves through a system. They describe two different ways of connecting these structures to achieve useful outcomes.

In one setup, several giant superatoms are closely linked in a specific arrangement. This allows them to pass quantum states between each other without decoherence, meaning no information is lost.

In another setup, the atoms are spaced farther apart but connected in a carefully tuned way so that waves remain synchronized. This makes it possible to direct quantum signals and distribute entanglement over long distances.

Superatoms and giant atoms are engineered systems that behave like atoms rather than naturally occurring ones.

A superatom is a quantum system made up of multiple natural atoms that share a single quantum state and respond to light as one entity.

A giant atom, on the other hand, connects to light or sound waves at several separate points in space. It is called "giant" because it is larger than the wavelength of the light it interacts with.

Giant atoms have defined energy levels and follow the rules of quantum mechanics, yet they can reach sizes of up to millimeters, making them visible to the naked eye. Through electromagnetic or acoustic waves, they can interact with their surroundings at multiple locations at the same time. One way to picture this is as a single atom linked to a wave at several distant points. This unusual setup allows the atom to be influenced by the waves it produces.

Materials provided by Chalmers University of Technology. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#11140. Co-owner of “endless” cruise hopes wealthy residents can buy ship back to restart travels

One of the co-owners of the operators of the “endless” Villa Vie Odyssey cruise hopes that residents are able to buy the ship back so they can continue with their travels.

Back on October 27, 200 travellers on board the Villa Vie Odyssey cruise were told that the “endless” adventure had come to an end. Residents who had paid $120,000 for a spot on board the ship were informed they had hours to pack their stuff and get off in Sihanoukville, Cambodia.

Villa Vie Residences, the cruise’s operator, blamed rising fuel costs for the ship’s “temporary layup,” stating that they would not be comfortable passing on the additional cost to passengers.

The ship has since been seized by authorities in Singapore, with reports claiming that it is “likely” to do with an unpaid fuel bill. However, there are also rumors that wealthy passengers might be lining up a bid for it at auction.

Speaking to CNN Travel, Villa Vie co-owner and executive chairman Mikael Petterson said he supports the rumored efforts.

“I support residents who are trying to raise capital and acquire the ship if it goes to auction, and I will assist in every way I can,” Petterson said. “Any successful acquisition would require the resident group to provide the equity and fund the costs of operating the company, using an approach similar to the one used in the past.

“There is no guarantee an acquisition will happen, but I hope they can develop a viable plan.”

“Endless” cruise ship seized by authorities after passengers abandoned in Cambodia

Couple spent $1 million on “endless” cruise before being told to leave in Cambodia

“Endless” cruise ship journey suddenly ends despite passengers selling homes to live on it

Petterson added that he also understands why residents “are angry and disappointed” by the situation with being kicked off the ship.

One couple spent $1 million between buying their spot on the ship and travelling around the world. They even sold their house to boost that budget as well. However, the Cassinghams told CNN they aren’t in any rush to rejoin the cruise if other passengers can rescue it.

Another woman spent close to $64,000 for a trip on the cruise, but didn’t even set foot on board, seeing the journey before her sailing date.

She doesn’t have any hopes about getting the money back, either.

#11139. How to Build an AI-Assisted Workflow That Does Not Leak

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

A generative AI tool can draft a client email, summarise a forty-message thread or refactor a function in seconds. That speed is real, and it explains how fast these tools have become part of the ordinary working day, used to summarise threads, draft replies and reformat data in a single paste. The same ease is the problem. It moves sensitive material into systems that were never scoped to hold it, quietly and at scale.

Here is the uncomfortable part for anyone running a lean team. The risk in that workflow is almost never the model being wrong. It is that nobody decided what should go into the tool in the first place, and nobody is accountable for where the data ends up. AI made the drafting instant. It did nothing on its own to make delegation safe.

This article is about the control that closes that gap. It is not a product and it is not a policy document. It is a person, placed deliberately in the workflow, making the one call that no model can make for you.

Samsung learned this in public. In 2023, engineers in its semiconductor division were given access to ChatGPT to help debug source code. Within weeks, staff had pasted proprietary code and internal meeting notes into the tool, and Samsung banned generative AI across the company soon after. The story ran everywhere, from Bloomberg to The Register. Nothing malfunctioned. People simply moved sensitive material into a system that was never scoped to hold it, and the material was gone.

Two things make this failure mode easy to hit, and both are structural rather than careless.

First, most of these tools run in the browser. The data loss prevention software a security team relies on was built to watch files, endpoints and network egress, and it often cannot see a paste action into a web app at all. Worse, much of this activity happens through personal accounts that sit outside the controls the organisation does have. The leak does not trip an alarm because nothing was downloaded and nothing was emailed. Text was typed into a box.

Second, consumer-grade accounts can retain what you give them. OpenAI's own documentation states that content from consumer ChatGPT may be used to improve its models unless the user turns that setting off. Paste a client list or a contract clause from the wrong account, and that material may now sit inside a training pipeline you do not control and cannot claw back.

For a developer or a founder, the lesson is not "do not use AI". It is that the point of failure sits one layer above the model, in how the work is handed off and who decided what was safe to hand over.

If any of the data moving through that workflow identifies a person, a client, a candidate, an employee or an investor, then GDPR treats the tools and people touching it as processors acting on your behalf. That sounds abstract until you translate it into design constraints, which is what a technical team can actually work with.

Under Article 28 of the GDPR, anyone who processes personal data for you must do so under a binding contract that sets out what is processed, for what purpose and for how long, and everyone handling that data must be under a confidentiality commitment. An AI vendor you have never papered is a processor you have not accounted for. A free account used quietly by one engineer is a sub-processor nobody signed off. The obligation does not disappear because the tool was convenient.

Location is the second constraint, and it is the one most teams miss. A person, contractor or tool processing your data outside the EU means personal data is being transferred outside the EU, which requires a specific safeguard such as the Standard Contractual Clauses on top of the Article 28 contract. Keep the procesing inside the EU and that entire category of transfer risk falls away before you have to manage it. For UK teams the position is the same: the Information Commissioner's Office sets out the identical core terms for a processor contract, documented instructions, confidentiality, security measures, rules on sub-processors, and deletion or return of data at the end of the engagement.

There is now a second regulatory layer as well. The EU AI Act, which entered into force in 2024, adds obligations on how AI systems are deployed and governed, on top of the data-protection duties GDPR already imposes. The detail is still settling, but the direction is clear: unmanaged, undocumented AI use is getting harder to defend, not easier. None of this is exotic. It is the same governance you would apply to any third party with access to your systems. AI simply introduced a new processor that most workflows never wrote down.

"Human in the loop" usually gets discussed as a quality measure: a person checks the output so errors do not ship. That is true, and it undersells the point badly. The human is also the only part of the workflow that can make a judgment about what should never enter the tool at all.

A model cannot decide that a board deck stays out of a consumer chatbot. A browser extension cannot know that one folder holds client data governed by a contract that forbids sub-processing without written consent. Those are judgment calls, and judgment is precisely the thing you cannot automate. The person doing the delegating is your policy enforcement point, sitting at the exact moment the decision is made. They decide what gets pasted, what gets redacted first, what runs only inside an enterprise account with training switched off, and what a human handles end to end because the risk is simply not worth the minutes saved.

This reframes the division of labour that actually holds up. The AI owns the mechanical layer: summarising a long thread, turning rough notes into a first draft, extracting action items, reformatting data. The human owns the judgment layer: what matters, how it is framed, and, above all, where the data is allowed to go. Remove the human and you have not automated the work. You have removed the only control that was reading the situation and deciding what was safe.

It is worth being specific about what that judgment looks like in practice, because it is a skill and not a job title. Someone exercising it knows which inputs are off limits without having to ask, keeps sensitive material inside controlled systems by default, and treats the question "should this go into the tool at all" as part of the work rather than an afterthought. Whether that person is an engineer, an operations lead or an executive assistant, the control is the same: someone with enough context to know what is sensitive, and enough standing to say no. That habit is what separates a workflow that is genuinely governed from one that merely has a policy document nobody reads.

The practical version, for a founder or a small technical team that wants the speed without the exposure:

Most teams can put the first four in place in about a week. The last two are harder, because they are not configuration changes. They are about whether the workflow still holds when the people in it change, and that is the part most teams discover they got wrong only after someone has already left.

The speed of AI is not in question. What AI cannot supply is the judgment about what should enter it, and the accountability for where the output goes. Treat the human in the loop as a security control, design the workflow around that control, and you keep the nine-second draft without the quiet data leak. Remove the human, and the only thing you have really automated is the leak.

#11136. Ex-police officer becomes VTuber to help stop viewers from committing crimes

A former Japanese police officer has become a VTuber, promising to share his experiences in patrol cars and riot police units to teach viewers about the law.

VTubers in Japan aren’t exactly strangers when it comes to collaborating with real-life law enforcement and government awareness campaigns. After all, Hololive’s AZKi was famously appointed a traffic regulation adviser and police station chief for a day by Kagawa Prefectural Police in March 2026.

However, it’s rarer for former police officers to leave their crime-fighting days behind and debut as a virtual creator. Well, that’s exactly what VTuber Sukenosuke Hongo has done, and the officer-turned-anime boy aims to share his practical advice with his viewers.

In his introduction video, which has garnered a whopping one million views since November 1, the former officer said he wants to teach his viewers about the law and how to protect themselves.

During his debut announcement, Sukenosuke singled out drivers as one group he hopes to help, explaining that his time responding to emergency calls showed him how often people struggle to handle these situations.

“When I responded to emergency calls and listened to people explain what happened, there were honestly plenty of times when I thought, ‘What is this person talking about?’” he said. “That’s why I want to teach people the correct information.”

Sukenosuke also aims to stop his viewers from becoming victims of crime as well, using his real-life experience working in “police boxes, patrol cars, and in riot police units” to keep fans more informed.

“The fundamental purpose of crime prevention is to stop you from becoming a victim in the first place,” said the VTuber. “Even if that next incident does happen, knowing how to respond properly makes it easier for the police to take action, too.”

#11134. This simple change stops robot swarms from getting stuck

Imagine a swarm of robots rushing to complete an urgent job, such as cleaning up an oil spill or assembling complex machinery. At first, adding more robots speeds things up. But after a certain point, the space becomes crowded, robots start interfering with one another, and overall progress slows.

This raises a simple but important question: in a limited area, how many robots can you deploy before efficiency starts to drop? Researchers at Harvard believe they have found a clear answer.

A new study from the lab of L. Mahadevan, the Lola England de Valpine Professor of Applied Mathematics, Organismic and Evolutionary Biology, and Physics, shows that adding a controlled amount of randomness to how robots move can reduce congestion and improve performance in crowded environments.

The work combines mathematical modeling, computer simulations, and real-world experiments. It demonstrates how basic local movement rules can lead to organized, efficient outcomes on a larger scale. The findings could influence how robotic fleets are designed and may even apply to human crowd management and traffic flow. The research was published in Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences and led by applied mathematics Ph.D. student Lucy Liu, with guidance from SEAS Senior Research Fellow Justin Werfel.

Why Randomness Helps Predict Complex Behavior

Studying dense crowds is difficult because individuals can take countless possible paths and interact in unpredictable ways, Liu explained. To simplify the problem, the researchers treated each robot as a basic unit with a small, adjustable amount of variation in its movement.

"This might be counterintuitive, because how could randomness make things easier to work with?" said Liu. "But in this case, when you have a lot of randomness, it becomes possible to take averages -- average distances, average times, average behaviors. This makes it a lot easier to make predictions."

To explore this idea, the team created computer simulations of robot groups, referred to as agents. Each agent started at a random location and was assigned a random destination. Once it reached its target, it immediately received a new one, mimicking continuous task assignment in real-world systems.

Each agent moved toward its goal with a tunable amount of variation, described as "noise." With no noise, agents moved in straight lines. With high noise, their paths became erratic and inefficient. However, this wandering also helped them navigate around one another.

The simulations revealed a clear pattern. When agents moved in perfectly straight paths, they quickly formed dense clusters and traffic jams that halted progress. When movement became too random, congestion disappeared but efficiency dropped due to excessive wandering.

Between these extremes, the researchers identified a sweet spot. In this range, agents occasionally bumped into one another and formed short-lived clusters, but still managed to slip past and keep moving. This balance allowed the system to maintain a steady flow.

Using these insights, the team developed formulas to estimate "goal attainment rate," or how many destinations are reached over time. These equations made it possible to determine the ideal combination of crowd density and movement randomness to maximize performance.

To confirm their findings, Liu collaborated with physicist Federico Toschi at Eindhoven University of Technology in the Netherlands. Together, they set up experiments with small wheeled robots in a lab equipped with an overhead camera.

Each robot carried a QR code so its position could be tracked and updated with new destinations. Although the physical robots moved more slowly and less precisely than the simulated agents, they displayed the same overall patterns.

The experiments supported a key idea: highly complex coordination does not require advanced intelligence or centralized control. Instead, simple local rules can produce effective group behavior, at least within certain density limits.

"Understanding how active matter, whether it is a swarm of ants, a herd of animals, or a group of robots, become functional and execute tasks in crowded environments using the principles of self-organization, is relevant to many questions in behavioral ecology," Mahadevan said. "Our study suggests strategies that might well be much broader than the instantiation we have focused on."

Liu noted that she has long been interested in designing safer and more efficient crowded spaces. This research points toward a future where the movement of large groups, whether robots, vehicles, or people, could be predicted and optimized using mathematical tools.

The results suggest that introducing controlled variability into movement patterns may improve flow in many real-world systems, from factory floors to city streets.

Funding for the research came from the National Science Foundation Graduate Research Fellowship Program under Grant No. DGE 2140743, along with grants from the Simons Foundation and the Henri Seydoux Fund.

Materials provided by Harvard John A. Paulson School of Engineering and Applied Sciences. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#11131. KFC is hosting a fake funeral so GTA 6 fans can take the day off

A KFC in Australia is hosting a funeral for a fake person, just so customers have a valid reason to take the day off work and play GTA 6.

With November 19 now just days away, the launch of Grand Theft Auto 6 is finally in reach. In a matter of hours, New Zealand-based players will be among the first to jump into Leonida and experience Jason and Lucia’s story.

As a result, plenty of fans have been plotting time off work, just so they don’t have any distractions. In the United States, the game is expected to cause $1 billion in disruption to the economy, with around 1.5 million people estimated to be taking the day off.

Not everyone has the time booked off, however, and some have been looking for other reasons to ditch work. Well, in Australia, one KFC is hosting a funeral, aimed at giving customers a legitimate excuse for taking the day off.

The restaurant, which is based in Robina, Queensland, is celebrating the life of ‘Ann Alibi’, and luckily enough, the ‘ceremony’ will start right as GTA 6 goes live.

“She was always there for us when we needed her. In sickness and in perfect health,” the event invite says. “By remarkable coincidence, her service will take place on the very same day something 13 years in the making finally arrives.”

KFC also added that “the service will include a hearse, coffin, floral tributes, a book of condolences, an order of service and even a heartfelt eulogy delivered by a surprise guest.”

A post shared by KFC Australia (@kfcaustralia)

Anyone who does show up for the ceremony will get free fried chicken, but that’ll mean pulling yourself away from the long-awaited game.

There are other opportunities for GTA fans to get rewarded for playing the game. One company is offering to pay someone $100,000 to be their official GTA VI Researcher.

Virgin Media Ireland is offering one lucky fan $900 to play the game for eight hours straight.

If you don’t succeed on either front, then you can take solace in the idea that the game will save you $5,000 should you decide to play it for over 50 days.

#11128. 17 Ideas From Educators to Start the School Year With Joy

Become a subscribing member today. Help us continue to bring “the science of a meaningful life” to you and to millions around the globe.

Greater Good Science Center • Magazine • In Action • In Education

Joy! What a beautiful way to start the school year, not only because it feels good, but also because science shows that joy is linked closely to learning, as well as individual and collective flourishing.

We asked Greater Good Education newsletter readers to share a favorite strategy for bringing joy into their school or classroom at the beginning of the year. The responses did not disappoint! Here are a few that span ages, time commitments, and subject areas.

Laura M. (Newark, DE): “Dancing it out” is a strategy that has been shown to reduce stress, release held feelings, and encourage kids to be in the moment. Providing time for activities like dancing is often seen as an “extra.” Building this activity into our weekly schedule has become a favorite stress-relieving activity—adding tremendous joy and connection between children and children and children and teachers.

Related Greater Good in Education (GGiE) practices: The World Is a Dance; Getting to Know Each Other Through Dance; We Are Better Dancing Together

Josh (New Haven, CT): Every Monday morning meeting, students greet each other by asking, “What brought you joy (made you smile/laugh) over the weekend?”

Laura H.: At the end of the day, with second graders, we share the activity from our day that brought the most joy. If I wasn’t there for it (i.e., recess, music, art, PE), I ask them to include details.

Related GGiE practices: Noticing Kindness; Giving Thanks; Looking for Gratitude in School; Sharing Gratitude Stories

Emi (Lobitos, Peru): Identifying forms of enchantment—I use this approach in our Women & Water Program. It’s an all-girls (ages 3–16) surf, science, and storytelling program based in Lobitos, a tiny fishing village in Northern Peru. We use “enchantment” as a way for young girls to foster their curiosity, creativity, and care for themselves, peers, community, and the coastal environment. We do this through photography (taking “macro” photos of small, wondrous things found in their environment, like a flower petal, a beetle, etc.). Or we do this in surfing—the girls notice the “awe” of riding the energy of a leftover storm until it crashes into the sand. These girls also made this song after an oil spill that rocked our community about two years ago.

Related GGiE practices: Meaningful Photos for Students; Mindfully Finding Awe in Nature

Angela (Pleasant Hill, CA): I am a school counselor and with my students who experience anxiety we use sidewalk chalk to write positive welcoming messages and pictures in front of our school. It makes others feel welcomed and helps my students feel connected to our school. They even told me it helps get them to school the first couple of days knowing they would see their chalk messages.

Related GGiE practices: Designing the Classroom to Promote Kindness; Kindness Buddy; Awe-Inspiring Affirmations

Lorien (St. Louis, MO): Have a “Would you rather…?” question on the whiteboard at the start of class—the funnier the better. Students put a checkmark next to their choice. It’s a fun way to welcome the “whole student self” into the classroom, not just their “learner selves.” Sometimes we talk about it, but often, we don’t.

Pam (Muscogee County, GA): To kick off the year, my eighth graders design and build a cardboard maze that guides three different rolling spheres from start to finish. Working in teams of four, students sketch a plan, build it with cardboard and hot glue, test it, and revise their design. Nothing works perfectly the first time. Students quickly discover that failure is a useful part of the design process. By the end of the week, teams are cheering when a ping pong ball, golf ball, or rubber ball finally makes it all the way through, and that joy comes from earning it through real trial and error. It sets the tone for the whole year: Engineering is a process of building, testing, and improving.

Kimberly (Williamsport, PA): Something that I introduce during the first week of classes is our 3 Good Things or 3 Little Wins Padlet. Each week students post about three good things or little wins they had during the week. Before the first post, we discuss why it is important to celebrate these things, no matter how small (e.g., a good cup of coffee!). We also discuss the impact that reflecting on these things has on our physical and psychological well-being. Each week I also post my wins for the week and comment on student posts as well. It creates a wonderful community!

Related GGIE practices: Three Good Things for Students; Gratitude Journals

Jonah (Duxbury, VT): As a high school English teacher, the first writing assignment I give to students asks them to choose a (clean) song that represents them and tell me why they feel connected to this song. I then make a playlist including all the students’ songs, and play it during work times or use it to play students’ walk-up song on special days like birthdays.

Related GGiE practices: Creating Musical Playlists for the Classroom; Prosocial Music Hub (13 practices to bring music into the classroom)

Joe (Bridgeport, CT): Students interact with each other as they navigate around the crowded classroom, connected only by cake-pop sticks balanced between each other using only their index finger. Fosters concentration, cooperation, joyful failures, interconnectedness, and intimacy without actually touching one another.

David (San Antonio, TX): For my introductory geology class, I work with the students to develop rules for a “rock-rock-rock” (igneous-sedimentary-metamorphic) version of “rock-paper-scissors.” Students develop the necessary hand signals and the action verbs that say what beats what. Once we have the rules established, the students engage in a best two out of three to crown the class champion. Branded university swag is available for the winners, and everyone gets a candy treat for participating.

Related GGiE practice: Making Science Meaningful

Susan (St. Paul, MN): I ask students to tell the rest of the class their name and a ridiculous lie about themselves—the more absurd the better. There’s always good-natured laughter and encouragement.

Harry (Hailey, ID): I work to end every “session” (class, administration meeting, faculty meeting, etc.) with laughter, whether incited by me or coming from someone else. That moment becomes the punctuation.

Related GGiE practices: Share a Laugh; The SEL 3 Signature Practices

Paige (Seattle, WA): About the second or third week of classes (graduate and undergraduate students) we begin doing this ritual that we do a few times during the semester: “The joy of contribution…I see this in you!” Students take turns identifying something they have genuinely observed in another student in the cohort/class: “One leadership capacity I see developing in you is________, because I watched you ________.” The BECAUSE matters.

Explore compassion, self-compassion, and awe with educators around the world.

Examples I have heard from pre-certified teachers and principals: “I see courage building in you because I have watched you ask questions the rest of us were afraid to ask.” OR, “I see you really owning belongingness as you consistently notice who is outside the conversation and what is happening around us as you read the room.”

Related GGiE practices: I See You. Everyone Matters; Behind Your Back

Lorien (St. Louis, MO): I use a guided visualization to prompt students to think about what joyful teaching, joyful learning, and joyful interactions look like, sound like, and feel like. It makes what we’re doing much more explicit, and it helps me know what they want from me and vice versa.

Related GGiE practice: Designing the Classroom to Promote Kindness

Angela (McMinnville, OR): A non-dominant hand drawing activity that gets us all out of our “perfection” thinking and into being learners…and laughing.

Related GGiE practice: Drawing as a Way to Manage Emotions

Milton (San Diego, CA): As the associate principal, I stand out in front of the school in the morning with a BIG SMILE sign on and greet the students with a “Good morning & have a great day!” Some students like a fist bump or handshake as well.

Related GGiE practice: Making Families Feel Welcome

Donna (Arkansas): “JOY” is the grounding rhythm that closes every day, honoring the joyful moments that JUST happened, recognizing community attunement through something an OTHER person did, and celebrating internal self-efficacy via something YOU did. Each person is free to select whichever letter resonates with them to share. We pass a rainstick around the circle; once it is turned to let its gentle cascade settle into silence, the person holding it speaks. This tactile ritual and quiet sound not only brings a profound sense of calm to the transition from classroom to home, but it also serves as a gentle reminder to carry those reflections outward.

Related GGiE practices: Inspiring Virtue With Moral Beauty; Stories of Moral Beauty; Noticing Kindness; Nurturing Students’ Wonder and Curiosity

Even in the face of incredible challenges, you are bringing so much joy to your students and their families, your colleagues, and yourself. May you all have a joyful start to the new school year!

#11127. Quantum AI just got shockingly good at predicting chaos

A new study led by researchers at UCL (University College London) shows that combining quantum computing with artificial intelligence can significantly improve predictions of complex physical systems over long periods. The hybrid approach outperforms leading models that rely only on conventional computers.

The results, published in Science Advances, could enhance simulations of how liquids and gases behave, known as fluid dynamics. These types of models are essential in fields such as climate science, transportation, medicine, and energy production.

The improved accuracy appears to come from how quantum computers process information. Unlike traditional computers that use bits set to either 1 or 0, quantum computers use qubits, which can exist as 1, 0, or anything in between. In addition, each qubit can influence others, allowing a relatively small number of qubits to represent an enormous number of possible states.

Professor Peter Coveney, senior author from UCL Chemistry and the Advanced Research Computing Centre, explained the challenge: "To make predictions about complex systems, we can either run a full simulation, which might take weeks -- often too long to be useful -- or we can use an AI model which is quicker but more unreliable over longer time scales.

"Our quantum-informed AI model means we could provide more accurate predictions quickly. Making predictions about fluid flow and turbulence is a fundamental science challenge but it also has many applications. Our method can be used in climate forecasting, in modeling blood flow and the interaction of molecules, or to better design wind farms so they generate more energy."

Although quantum computers are widely expected to surpass classical machines in power, their real-world use has so far been limited. This new approach integrates quantum computing into a specific stage of the AI training process.

Typically, AI models learn from large datasets generated by simulations or observations. In this case, the data is first processed by a quantum computer, which identifies key statistical patterns that remain stable over time. These patterns, known as invariant statistical properties, are then used to guide the training of an AI model running on a conventional supercomputer.

The quantum-informed AI system delivered about 20 percent greater accuracy compared to standard AI models that did not use quantum-derived patterns. It also maintained stable predictions over longer periods, even when modeling chaotic systems.

Another major advantage was efficiency. The method required hundreds of times less memory, making it far more practical for large-scale simulations.

This performance boost comes from two defining features of quantum computing. Entanglement allows qubits to influence each other regardless of distance, while superposition enables a qubit to exist in multiple states at once until measured. Together, these properties allow quantum systems to process vast amounts of information in a compact form.

Demonstrating Practical Quantum Advantage

First author Maida Wang of the UCL Centre for Computational Science said: "Our new method appears to demonstrate 'quantum advantage' in a practical way -- that is, the quantum computer outperforms what is possible through classical computing alone. These findings could inspire the development of novel classical approaches that achieve even higher accuracy, though they would likely lack the remarkable data compression and parameter efficiency offered by our method. The next steps are to scale up the method using larger datasets and to apply it to real-world situations which typically involve even more complexity. In addition, a provable theoretical framework will be proposed."

Co-first author Xiao Xue, from Advanced Research Computing at UCL, added: "In this work, we demonstrate for the first time that quantum computing can be meaningfully integrated with classical machine learning methods to tackle complex dynamical systems, including fluid mechanics. It is exciting to see this kind of 'quantum-informed' approach moving towards practical use."

The researchers suggest that quantum computers are particularly well suited to modeling these systems because they can compactly represent their underlying physics. Many complex systems behave in ways that resemble quantum effects, where changes in one region can influence distant parts of the system, similar to entanglement.

Overcoming Limits of Current Quantum Hardware

Current quantum computers face challenges such as noise, errors, and interference, which often require large numbers of measurements. The new method avoids these issues by using the quantum computer only once during the workflow, rather than repeatedly exchanging data between quantum and classical systems.

The study used a 20-qubit IQM quantum computer connected to powerful classical computing resources at the Leibniz Supercomputing Centre in Germany.

To function, quantum computers must operate at extremely low temperatures, around minus 273C (close to absolute zero, colder than anything in space).

The research was funded by UCL and the UK's Engineering and Physical Sciences Research Council (EPSRC), with additional support from IQM Quantum Computers and the Leibniz Supercomputing Centre in Munich.

As researchers continue to scale up this approach, it could open the door to more accurate and efficient predictions across a wide range of scientific and engineering applications.

Materials provided by University College London. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#11126. Taco Bell’s launches new Baja Blast alongside Naked Chicken Chalupa return

Taco Bell is expanding its drink lineup with a new zero-sugar Mountain Dew flavor while preparing to bring back one of its most recognizable menu items.

The fast food chain officially launched Mountain Dew Baja Midnight Zero Sugar nationwide on November 12, offering customers a new version of its passionfruit-flavored fountain drink.

The release comes alongside plans to bring the Naked Chicken Chalupa back to restaurants across the US.

The returning Chalupa, which replaces a traditional shell with crispy chicken, has appeared on Taco Bell’s menu several times since its 2017 debut, including on the chain’s 2016 Decades menu in August 2026.

Mountain Dew Baja Midnight Zero Sugar features the same passionfruit-forward flavor as the original Baja Midnight, but without sugar or calories.

The new beverage is available nationwide in three sizes, with a small priced at $2.59, a medium at $2.79, and a large at $2.99.

Rather than using a traditional fried flatbread shell, the chicken itself holds the Chalupa’s fillings, which include shredded lettuce, diced tomatoes, cheddar cheese, and creamy Avocado Ranch Sauce.

The returning menu item will be priced at $5.99, although Taco Bell has not provided a specific nationwide release date in the announcement.

Both additions continue Taco Bell’s ongoing menu updates, with Baja Midnight Zero Sugar already available at participating restaurants and the Naked Chicken Chalupa expected to follow.

#11123. How to Build a Habit by Starting Ridiculously Small

Become a subscribing member today. Help us continue to bring “the science of a meaningful life” to you and to millions around the globe.

Greater Good Science Center • Magazine • In Action • In Education

In 75 minutes a week, I can reap the benefits of listening to uplifting music, getting out in nature, and getting morning outdoor light, all while exercising my dogs and getting enough cardio to measurably improve my health, happiness, and longevity.

And at times in my life, I have been a real runner. I ran a marathon in my 20s and a couple of half-marathons in my 30s. In my 40s, I mostly just made spectacular plans every January and September: I’d find exciting destination races to train for, join a gym and a yoga studio, sign up for running challenges, and pick out elaborate training plans. I’d run three or four days a week for a week or two, and then nada. Zippo. Zero runs per week.

Then, in 2020, I got into a real running habit. I’ve told the longer version of this story on a TED stage, so I won’t repeat it here, but suffice it to say that I did it by doing very, very little running. When I started going to Orange Theory classes, I kept running. I sustained this exercise habit for six years, through breast cancer and many other difficult life events.

Then the Orange Theory studio down the street from me closed. I tried other studios, but the classes I like are impossible to get into.

So I embraced working out on my own and getting back to running outside with my dogs. I bought very heavy dumbbells and set up a home gym on our covered porch. I spent an inordinate amount of time choosing a destination race, enlisting my adult children to join me. I set up a training schedule on my Garmin watch, and when I didn’t stick to it, I used Nike Run Club. I made a running playlist. I bought a double running leash.

Friends, I have done four runs in three weeks.

I know what I need to do. It’s so wildly unambitious that I’m embarrassed to tell you. I just need to get dressed to run first thing in the morning, put my dogs on their double leash, put on my playlist, and walk out the door, down the driveway, and to the mailbox. That’s it.

I know that most days, I’ll get a walk in, and once that walk is familiar, I’ll sometimes run a little. Some days I might run more, without even deciding to. But even when I don’t feel like doing more, the morning light I get while retrieving the mail and the walk back up our steep driveway are both good for me and genuinely enjoyable. It’s way better than nothing.

The mistake I make again and again is assuming that the size of my plan and the odds of my success are related: the more inspiring the goal and the more elaborate the plan, the better my results will be.

Ambition and results are related, of course, but not in the direction you think.

Creating a habit takes energy and focus, and we’ve only got so much of either, which is why ambitious plans produce a brilliant week and then…nothing. It takes between 18 and 254 days for a behavior to become automatic, and what predicts faster adoption isn’t ambition, self-discipline, or willpower; it’s repetition.

Repetition in a consistent context drives habit formation. Not just effort, intensity, or how badly someone wants it. Doing the thing, in the same place at about the same time, again and again, has the most impact.

Eli Susman, Ph.D., joins me in a webinar in a few weeks to talk about his book Micropractice.

Microhabits: The Science of Habits So Small They Actually Stick

Success is motivating. Doing what you said you’d do, even something absurdly small, builds the belief that you can do it. Failing repeatedly does the opposite; that’s a big part of why I spent my 40s making elaborate plans but not running.

Small behaviors make repetition more likely. What separates an actual habit from a nice couple of weeks is whether it holds up when you’re stressed, tired, busy, or not feeling 100%.

Missing a single day doesn’t meaningfully set most people back. We don’t need to be perfect here, folks. What won’t survive is a plan so ambitious that the off days outnumber the on days.

Micropractices, as researcher Eli Susman calls them, are very small behaviors that also have their own benefits; a tiny practice isn’t just a placeholder for a “real” one. Susman found that 20 seconds of self-compassionate touch (for example, sending kindness to yourself while placing your hand on your heart) per day improved mental health and lowered stress compared with a placebo finger tapping practice.

Across 280,000 meditation sessions in 103 countries, how consistently people meditated predicted their mood and resilience better than how long they sat. Similarly, a meta-analysis combining the results of many studies found that the benefits of practicing breathwork for stress didn’t depend on how long the session were. And breaking up six hours of sitting with brief bouts of walking improves energy, mood, and food cravings more than doing 30 minutes of walking all at once.

I hate the idea of abandoning my big plans. But the truth is that I am not choosing between the ambitious version and the tiny version. I am choosing between the tiny version and nothing.

Break the core behavior central to your habit down into something you can do in under five minutes, start to finish. Under a minute is even better. This isn’t the version that would impress your doctor or your neighbors; it’s the version you can do while exhausted, while traveling, while a little bit sick, and when you want nothing more than to stay on the couch.

Then, check it for resistance. If you feel any reluctance when you imagine doing it, it isn’t small enough yet. Cut it in half. Most people need to start with a micropractice: putting on your walking shoes and stepping out the front door, doing one pushup or 30 seconds of breathing, eating a single leaf of romaine, which, for the record, contains actual fiber and actual nutrients.

Do just that for a while. Resist the urge to do more. This is hard, but the moment you’re no longer willing to be unambitious is the moment you put the whole thing at risk. That’s when difficulty enters, and negotiation with yourself, and the possibility of failure. Your tiny habit doesn’t depend on motivation, which is precisely what makes it survive the days when you have none.

Expand only when you’re itching to, and only in absurdly small increments. Go one more minute, not 10.

Every time you repeat a behavior, your brain physically strengthens the connections that produce it, the way feet crossing a meadow gradually wear a path. Repeat it enough and the path becomes a groove your brain follows on its own, no willpower required. You’re carving a neural pathway for your habit, and everything you want is downstream from that.

At some point, it will become uncomfortable not to do this new behavior. You’ll do it without deciding to, without trying to. That’s the whole goal: repetition until the thing runs itself.

It’s hard to abandon our grand plans and great ambitions for something embarrassingly small. But, paradoxically, it is only in that tiny step that some of our grand plans are truly born.

Join Christine Carter and Eli Susman on Oct. 15 for a free live conversation, with a few tiny habits to try together.

Christine Carter, Ph.D. is a Senior Fellow at the Greater Good Science Center. She is the author of The New Adolescence: Raising Happy and Successful Teens in an Age of Anxiety and Distraction (BenBella, 2020), The Sweet Spot: How to Accomplish More by Doing Less (Ballantine Books, 2015), and Raising Happiness: 10 Simple Steps for More Joyful Kids and Happier Parents (Random House, 2010). A former director of the GGSC, she served for many years as author of its parenting blog, Raising Happiness. Find out more about Christine here.

#11121. Free browser game lets you play poker with Wikipedia articles

A free browser game called Wikdraw is putting a bizarre twist on poker by replacing traditional playing cards with Wikipedia articles, challenging players to find connections between completely unrelated subjects.

Created by tolecrien, Wikdraw takes the familiar five-card draw format and swaps out suits and numbers for everything from Hollywood celebrities and historical figures to countries, animals, and scientific concepts.

The goal is simple: build the strongest possible poker hand by finding things your five Wikipedia articles have in common. There’s no betting involved, and it’s a single-player game, meaning it’s all about your own high scores.

Players start with five cards from a 15-card deck and have two opportunities to discard unwanted cards and draw replacements. However, unlike traditional poker, you’re looking for shared characteristics rather than matching numbers.

For example, five actors could form a flush, while three historical figures from the same country and two animals could potentially make a full house.

@taksin_glock19 beat this pic.twitter.com/pDNCeBe5hV

What makes Wikdraw particularly interesting is its scoring system, which awards points based on how unusual the connections between cards are.

Common traits, such as being American or born in the same century, are worth very little. However, more specific connections can earn significantly higher scores.

In one example, pairing Shaquille O’Neal with Olivia Rodrigo earns 35 points because both are associated with Interscope Records. Their shared status as singers adds another 13 points, while simply being American earns nothing.

Another hand featuring Israel, the British Raj, Sri Lanka, a camel, and a donkey can form a full house thanks to shared geographical connections between the first three cards and similarities between the two animals.

Wendy’s free RPG rewards players with real food for beating bosses and is 15 hours long

Addictive browser game turns Teamfight Tactics into Dragon Ball Z

Wikipedia gacha game turns random pages into Pokemon cards you can collect

The game even rewards multiple overlapping connections, meaning a single pair of cards can earn points for several different traits.

Players also receive bonuses for their final hand, including 150 points for a flush, 100 for four of a kind, and 75 for a full house.

The catch is that the game doesn’t reveal all the connections until the round ends, leaving players to figure out which seemingly unrelated Wikipedia pages might actually go together.

Wikdraw offers a daily challenge featuring the same 15 cards for everyone, although they’re shuffled differently for each player. There’s also a random mode for anyone wanting to keep playing.

It’s not the first time Wikipedia has been turned into an unlikely card game, either. Earlier in 2026, Wikigacha transformed Wikipedia articles into collectible Pokemon-style cards, complete with booster packs and PvP battles.

Wikdraw is completely free to play at wikdraw.org, with no download required.

#11120. Think AI "knows" what it’s doing? Scientists say think again

These are everyday words people use to describe what goes on in the human mind. But when those same terms are applied to artificial intelligence, they can unintentionally make machines seem more human than they really are.

"We use mental verbs all the time in our daily lives, so it makes sense that we might also use them when we talk about machines -- it helps us relate to them," said Jo Mackiewicz, professor of English at Iowa State. "But at the same time, when we apply mental verbs to machines, there's also a risk of blurring the line between what humans and AI can do."

Mackiewicz and Jeanine Aune, a teaching professor of English and director of the advanced communication program at Iowa State, are part of a research team that studied how writers describe AI using human-like language. This type of wording, known as anthropomorphism, assigns human traits to non-human systems. Their study, "Anthropomorphizing Artificial Intelligence: A Corpus Study of Mental Verbs Used with AI and ChatGPT," was published in Technical Communication Quarterly.

The research team also included Matthew J. Baker, associate professor of linguistics at Brigham Young University, and Jordan Smith, assistant professor of English at the University of Northern Colorado. Both previously studied at Iowa State University.

Why Human-Like Language About AI Can Be Misleading

According to the researchers, using mental verbs to describe AI can create a false impression. Words such as "think," "know," "understand," and "want" suggest that a system has thoughts, intentions, or awareness. In reality, AI does not possess beliefs or feelings. It produces responses by analyzing patterns in data, not by forming ideas or making conscious decisions.

Mackiewicz and Aune also pointed out that this kind of language can overstate what AI is capable of. Phrases like "AI decided" or "ChatGPT knows" can make systems seem more independent or intelligent than they actually are. This can lead to unrealistic expectations about how reliable or capable AI is.

There is also a broader concern. When AI is described as if it has intentions, it can distract from the humans behind it. Developers, engineers, and organizations are responsible for how these systems are built and used.

"Certain anthropomorphic phrases may even stick in readers' minds and can potentially shape public perception of AI in unhelpful ways," Aune said.

How News Writers Actually Use AI Language

To better understand how often this kind of language appears, the researchers analyzed the News on the Web (NOW) corpus. This massive dataset contains more than 20 billion words from English-language news articles published in 20 countries.

They focused on how frequently mental verbs such as "learns," "means," and "knows" were used alongside terms like AI and ChatGPT.

Mental Verbs Are Less Common Than Expected

The study found that news writers do not frequently pair AI-related terms with mental verbs.

While anthropomorphism is common in everyday speech, it appears far less often in news writing. "Anthropomorphism has been shown to be common in everyday speech, but we found there's far less usage in news writing," Mackiewicz said.

Among the examples identified, the word "needs" appeared most often with AI, showing up 661 times. For ChatGPT, "knows" was the most frequent pairing, but it appeared only 32 times.

The researchers noted that editorial standards may play a role. Associated Press guidelines, which discourage attributing human emotions or traits to AI, could be influencing how journalists write about these technologies.

Context Matters More Than the Words Themselves

Even when mental verbs were used, they were not always anthropomorphic.

For instance, the word "needs" often described basic requirements rather than human-like qualities. Phrases such as "AI needs large amounts of data" or "AI needs some human assistance" are similar to how people describe non-human systems like cars or recipes. In these cases, the language does not imply that AI has thoughts or desires.

In other cases, "needs" was used to express what should be done, such as "AI needs to be trained" or "AI needs to be implemented." Aune explained that these examples were often written in passive voice, which shifts responsibility back to human actors rather than the technology itself.

The study also showed that not all uses of mental verbs are equal. Some phrases move closer to suggesting human-like qualities.

For example, statements like "AI needs to understand the real world" can imply expectations tied to human reasoning, ethics, or awareness. These uses go beyond simple descriptions and begin to suggest deeper capabilities.

"These instances showed that anthropomorphizing isn't all-or-nothing and instead exists on a spectrum," Aune said

Overall, the researchers found that anthropomorphism in news coverage is both less frequent and more nuanced than many might assume.

"Overall, our analysis shows that anthropomorphization of AI in news writing is far less common -- and far more nuanced -- than we might think," Mackiewicz said. "Even the instances that did anthropomorphize AI varied widely in strength."

The findings highlight the importance of context. Simply counting words is not enough to understand how language shapes meaning.

"For writers, this nuance matters: the language we choose shapes how readers understand AI systems, their capabilities and the humans responsible for them," Mackiewicz said.

The research team also emphasized that these insights can help professionals think more carefully about how they describe AI in their work.

"Our findings can help technical and professional communication practitioners reflect on how they think about AI technologies as tools in their writing process and how they write about AI," the research team wrote in the published study.

As AI continues to develop, the way people talk about it will remain important. Mackiewicz and Aune said writers will need to stay mindful of how word choices influence perception.

Looking ahead, the team suggested that future studies could explore how different words shape understanding and whether even rare uses of anthropomorphic language have a strong impact on how people view AI.

Materials provided by Iowa State University. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#11118. How to Learn Java with AI: A Practical Guide for Beginners

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Java has been one of the most in-demand programming languages for over two decades. It powers Android apps, banking systems, enterprise software, and huge backend platforms. The good news: learning it has never been easier. With AI tools like Claude, ChatGPT, and GitHub Copilot, you now have a patient tutor available at any hour.

The catch is that AI can help you learn faster or help you avoid learning at all. This guide shows you how to use it the right way.

Traditionally, learning Java meant reading a textbook, watching videos, and getting stuck on a compiler error for two hours with nobody to ask. AI removes much of that friction:

Instant explanations. Ask "what is the difference between an abstract class and an interface?" and get an answer pitched at your level.

Personalized pace. Ask for the same concept explained three different ways until it clicks.

Error decoding. Paste a confusing stack trace and get a plain-English explanation of what went wrong.

Unlimited practice. Generate fresh exercises on any topic, any time.

But AI is a tutor, not a replacement for practice. You still have to write the code yourself.

Before writing code, you need the JDK (Java Development Kit) and an IDE such as IntelliJ IDEA, Eclipse, or VS Code.

"I'm a complete beginner on Windows. Walk me through installing the JDK and IntelliJ IDEA, and help me run my first Hello World program."

If something goes wrong (and it often does with PATH variables), paste the exact error message and ask what it means.

Use AI to build understanding with analogies and examples.

"Explain Java loops like I'm a beginner. Give me one real-world analogy, then three short examples that get progressively harder."

Then close the chat and write your own loop from memory. That second step is where learning actually happens.

OOP is where many beginners struggle, and where AI shines as a tutor. Concepts like classes, objects, inheritance, polymorphism, encapsulation, and abstraction are easier to grasp when you can ask follow-up questions.

"Teach me inheritance in Java using a Vehicle, Car, and Bike example. Then quiz me with 5 questions, one at a time, and wait for my answer before giving feedback."

Asking the AI to quiz you, rather than just explain, turns passive reading into active recall.

Once you've written a program, ask AI to review it. This is one of the most valuable habits you can build.

"Review my Java code. Point out bugs, style issues, and anything that isn't idiomatic. Explain why each suggestion matters, but don't rewrite the whole thing for me."

That last sentence is important. You want feedback and understanding, not a finished solution you copy and paste.

When your code throws a NullPointerException or ArrayIndexOutOfBoundsException, resist the urge to say "fix this." Instead, try:

"Here's my code and the error. Don't give me the answer yet. Ask me guiding questions so I can find the bug myself."

Debugging is a core professional skill. Using AI as a Socratic coach builds that skill instead of bypassing it.

Tutorials only take you so far. Projects are where Java sticks. Ask AI to suggest projects matched to your level:

Beginner: Number guessing game, to-do list in the console, simple calculator

Intermediate: Library management system, bank account simulator, student record manager

Advanced: REST API with Spring Boot, inventory system with a database, a chat application

"I know Java basics and OOP. Suggest a project I can finish in a weekend, break it into milestones, and don't write the code for me."

Copy-pasting without understanding. If you can't explain a line of code, you haven't learned it. Ask the AI to explain it line by line.

Trusting AI blindly. AI can produce code that looks right but is wrong, outdated, or insecure. Always run it, test it, and verify against official documentation.

Skipping the struggle. A little frustration is productive. Try for 15 to 20 minutes before asking for help.

Never writing code from scratch. Regularly practice without AI so you know what you can actually do on your own, especially before job interviews.

AI is a fantastic assistant, but it can't tell you what you don't know you need to learn. A structured course gives you a proven roadmap, so you cover the right topics in the right order, while AI fills in the gaps, answers your questions, and keeps you moving.

The best way to learn Java with AI is to treat it like a good mentor: ask it to explain, challenge, and review, but do the typing yourself. Learners who combine consistent practice with smart AI use progress faster than ever before.

#11116. Seven Ways to Outlast Political Authoritarianism

Become a subscribing member today. Help us continue to bring “the science of a meaningful life” to you and to millions around the globe.

Greater Good Science Center • Magazine • In Action • In Education

Though Djurdja Padejski was raised in communist Yugoslavia, her life there felt stable and uneventful for a long time. In the 1980s, under Yugoslavia’s “soft” one-party government, Padejski and her friends read Western literature, watched Hollywood movies, and listened to the BBC.

“We grew up in a very comfortable, naive environment,” Padejski says.

That changed in the 1990s when Yugoslavia split into several republics. Civil war broke out, and a virulent strain of nationalism took hold in Serbia, the Yugoslavian state where Padejski lived. Firebrand leader Slobodan Milošević ascended to power and began meddling in elections, targeting opposition leaders, and igniting ethnic cleansing campaigns.

“He captured the entire society and his party,” Padejski remembers. “Very soon I found myself on the streets protesting.” Padejski’s activism against repressive Serbian regimes would continue until 2011, when she and her husband, investigative journalist Djordje Padejski, left the country after people threatened to kill them.

The Padejskis arrived in the U.S. with hard-won knowledge of how to outlast repression over the long haul. In recent years, they’ve tapped back into that knowledge and shared it with those around them as they witness here in America the illegal destruction of government agencies, election tampering, and warrantless arrests of citizens and non-citizens. This isn’t just true in the United States—around the world, democracy is declining.

How can we go on defying rising authoritarianism when no clear endpoint is in sight? Psychology and motivation research complement the experience of people like the Padejskis by addressing how to conserve energy and sustain commitment to your ideals in challenging conditions.

“The goal of the authoritarian regime is to desensitize us to extremes, to things we should never agree with or consider normal,” says Djurdja Padejski, now a language and culture researcher (and doctoral candidate) at Arizona State University.

Surviving repression depends on rejecting this presumed “normality” and refusing to let your inner state mirror extremes imposed from above. To that end, here are seven research-backed ways to subsist under indefinite duress.

Life under repression feels exhausting due to the colossal scale of so much of what is happening: attacks on the free press, denial of life-saving aid to millions overseas, ongoing ICE killings on the streets and in detention facilities. Reading about such developments understandably triggers feelings of overwhelm. Research finds that these feelings can drive people to shut down their emotions, dampening their motivation to help in concrete ways.

Djurdja Padejski has come to recognize that to avoid that trap, she has to face her human limits squarely without drowning in self-recrimination. As a young adult under Milošević’s rule, she learned early that worrying about what she couldn’t do was a waste of time and energy.

“It is very important not to get stuck in that moment, not to be clutching your pearls,” she says. “We have to be strategic to preserve our resources.”

Admitting your own lack of control over the big picture can be a necessary prelude to taking the actions still available to you, Padejski says. “We have to see [the situation] for what it is, so we can focus our efforts on keeping our minds, our bodies, and our communities as healthy as possible under the circumstances.”

Once you’ve gotten clear about what is within your power to control, you can get granular by carrying out regular acts of “micro-resistance.” These acts of defiance do not require you to upend your daily rhythms or put your life on the line. They can be as simple as attending a march, supporting a neighbor at an immigration court visit, or wearing a shirt with a resistance slogan.

Micro-resistance acts—such as creating protest art and continuing to study even when their department was banned from the university—helped sustain both Padejskis through years of repression, reminding them of the power they had despite limits imposed from on high.

“These micro-level actions add up,” Djordje Padejski writes. “Even small acts of defiance can maintain your sense of agency.”

Self-described “small-time” lawyer Daniel Suitor excels at this kind of micro-resistance. During ICE raids in his hometown of Minneapolis, he channeled his overwhelm by signing up for a course on how to file legal paperwork to free unjustly detained Twin Cities residents.

After Suitor filed his first case, he found out surprisingly quickly that he’d won and that the man he was representing would be freed. That proof-of-concept case spurred him to file another legal petition, then several more in quick succession. And he wasn’t alone in his commitment: To date, Minnesota lawyers have filed more than 1,000 cases challenging unjust detentions.

Acts of micro-resistance can also help prime us for future courageous action. When people perform smaller acts of selflessness and service, large-scale heroism becomes easier to contemplate, explains Medical College of Wisconsin heroism researcher Zeno Franco. Concrete practice can make you more adept at translating abstract ideals into action.

“You need to understand beyond theory,” Djurdja Padejski says. “We actually, as humans, can’t imagine how concepts work in [the] real world unless we experience them hands-on.”

For the Padejskis, one of the most stifling aspects of life under Serbian autocracy was not knowing when the repression would lift, if at all.

Periods of confinement, literal or figurative, are more bearable when a clear endpoint is in sight, and indefinite periods of repression feel uniquely oppressive. A third or more of incarcerated people with open-ended sentences suffer from depression. Others feel rooted to the present moment, severed from any potential future.

“I simply try to live in the here and now and not to plan,” one prisoner told the University of Bern researcher Irene Marti, who studies open-ended confinement. “Because I don’t have any future or only one that is very far away.”

When the endpoint of repression is uncertain, or when a challenging situation has no clear natural limit, creating your own intermediate endpoints can help you stay committed to your goals and values.

In a recent goal-setting study, when people broke an annual community service goal into granular sub-goals (say, four hours of service a week, or eight every two weeks), they volunteered more hours in the long run than people without intermediate goals.

In the same way, committed resisters can pledge to put in a set number of hours each week, or carry out a set number of clearly defined actions. According to self-determination theory, choosing and pursuing your own accessible goals boosts your well-being and motivation—internal changes that persist even when the larger crisis remains ongoing.

As governments around the world curtail freedom of speech and movement, it’s hard not to feel the noose tightening. One way to create more existential breathing room is to consider how this moment fits into the longer arc of history.

This helps you subsist in the present by reminding you of parallel past moments (whether decades or centuries ago), how they came to a head, and what ultimately resolved them. Research finds that when people take a long-term perspective, reflecting on recent stressful events as though they happened far in the past, they report less emotional distress in the moment.

Djurdja Padejski found solace in doing this during Milošević’s reign. “I survived that period mostly by reading the columns in the newspapers,” she says. One column in particular, by Serbian anthropologist Ivan Čolović, examined the regime’s activities from a broader historical and social perspective.

“He was offering a vocabulary and a theoretical apparatus to help us deal with the horrors of civil war. The news about it was the most severely censored, the propaganda was relentless, and all of it was new for many of us who were children when the fallout of our world began,” Padejski says. “That’s how I survived with my sanity intact, because it helped me to recognize what is happening to society.”

Among the most potent present-day energy drains is the social media industrial complex that feeds on people’s rage and frustration, serving up extreme viewpoints that deepen entrenched social divides as they rack up likes and views.

“Social media and internet platforms—they’re amplifying what is also a typical quality of authoritarian regimes. They’re all about grandiosity,” says Djurdja Padejski, who studies the linguistic mechanisms of propaganda. “Algorithms grab people’s attention by intensifying the language and the visuals and the overall framing.” That’s a big reason why frequent social media use saps your cognitive resources and motivation, making it harder to subsist under repression.

Plugging this energy drain requires not just limiting your time online, but redirecting more of that time to the in-person, small-group exchange to which humans are uniquely suited.

When you engage with people face to face, it’s much harder to reduce them to a stereotype or particular viewpoint. And by getting to know them in all their messiness, contradiction, and complexity, you bond with them in ways that lighten the mental and practical load for everyone involved.

“To resist this reduction of our existence to, ‘What are you for or against?’ means embracing creativity, art, friends, communal projects, protests, walks,” Padejski says. “These 10 years of resistance [in Serbia]—I honestly remember it as a series of walks. Sometimes that meant running away together from Milošević‘s militias or the official police, or from water cannons, or rubber bullets. But what is left is our conversations on ideas, on creations.”

Though exchanges like these may not further resistance directly, they form the edifice that sustains it. Within bonded human-scale communities, members have a clear sense of not just their own energetic capacity, but that of others in the group. That means they can pinch-hit for one another: When someone needs help, they can fill in on the spot—or, like relay racers, take up the baton when someone needs to withdraw. This shared flexibility and responsiveness help all group members to stay committed for the long haul.

For the Padejskis, subsisting under Serbian repression meant homing in on the values that were most important to them, and upholding those values even if it meant sacrificing convenience or comfort. When Djordje Padejski needed a lifesaving drug, a relative told him he could work his connections to obtain the medicine, but only if Padejski joined the Serbian dictator’s political party. Padejski refused, knowing that doing so put his health at risk.

The couple have continued that tradition in the U.S., though in somewhat less life-altering ways. After Elon Musk unleashed an apparent Nazi salute at a Trump post-inauguration event, the Padejskis gave up their Tesla. “I drive a Subaru now,” Djurdja Padejski says. “I really don’t like it compared to Tesla, but I don’t regret it.”

Some people write off such moral stands as more symbolic than substantive. But symbol, it turns out, shapes substance: What you stand for predicts and influences what you’ll do down the road, for good or ill. When people have a clear, consistent moral identity, studies find, they’re more likely to take real-world action aligned with that identity.

On the other hand, when you’re willing to cave on something that seems minor, like supporting a complicit business, “you are detached from your inner beliefs, from what you know is real and good and safe,” Djurdja Padejski says. “Once you’re there, you are ready to compromise a lot of values that you think you are really one with.”

One unexpected thing the Padejskis learned, under Milošević and afterward, was the importance of not swinging into action every time a devastating piece of news hit. Overcommitment amounted to self-sabotage, sapping energy and resources they’d need later on.

Military commanders have long known the importance of “keeping their powder dry,” Zeno Franco points out, holding back so they can act at the moment of maximum impact. In the same way, the best long-distance runners often hang back throughout a course to conserve energy, then uncork a burst of speed in the race’s final minutes.

Djurdja Padejski followed this principle to great effect under Milošević. She bided her time leading up to the critical elections of 2000, then worked tirelessly as an election volunteer to monitor for irregularities and make sure no one was falsifying results. All the while, ruling party members with guns lurked in the background.

“This guy started opening his gun and checking the bullets, and we didn’t cave. We just ignored him,” Padejski says. “We knew we were risking our lives, but we really believed it’s now or never.” Serbia’s democratic opposition party ultimately won that election, causing Milošević’s downfall.

Padejski hopes democratic activists around the world will take a similar approach as crucial elections near. Under repressive conditions, keeping your powder dry “is not an instruction to put off action as long as possible, but rather the wisdom to know that being prepared to act at just the right moment may require careful guarding of resources and energy,” Franco says. “Biding your time is not the same as surrender.”

Elizabeth Svoboda is the author of What Makes a Hero?: The Surprising Science of Selflessness. Her upcoming book, The Art of Pacing, is about setting a sustainable pace in a speed-obsessed world.
