#11114. Birdwatchers are buying GTA 6 just to look at birds

GTA 6 hype has even reached the birdwatching community, where some players are already planning to put the heists on hold and see how many birds they can spot.

With GTA 6 launching on November 19, the wait for the first new game in the series in over a decade is almost over. It’s already expected to break sales records when it drops and could cost the US as much as $1 billion in sick days as fans abandon work to play.

But it’s not just video game players who are ready to lose hours in Vice City, even birdwatchers are pumped for the release.

GTA 6 reportedly has 62 types of birds to find and record in a wildlife logbook, giving birdwatchers plenty of reasons to explore Leonida with a pair of binoculars.

A November 15 post on r/birding picked up more than 1,700 upvotes after one user asked if any other birdwatchers were looking forward to the game. “I think the crime sprees are going to have to wait,” they wrote.

It turns out they were far from alone, as plenty of fellow birders replied, saying they were ready to take their hobby into GTA 6.

“I’m so hyped. Probably the first thing I do if we’re given the binoculars right away,” said one reply.

“My birding group is planning to stream the week after it comes out,” added another. “We streamed Red Dead last year, and we’re hoping there’ll be tons of birds to spot for GTA.”

GTA 6 forces you to take your time with the main story instead of speedrunning it

GTA 6 will feature over 170 different animals including “Legendary” ones

Police can’t wait for GTA 6 to be released so they can “catch a break”

“I feel like such a dork being excited for the wildlife in a GTA game of all places but I truly am excited to see it all LOL,” wrote a third.

This isn’t the first time that a Rockstar game has appealed to birdwatchers either, as many commented that Red Dead Redemption 2 got them into the hobby.

“RDR2 flipped the birding switch on for me. I have identified actual birds irl because I saw them in the game,” said one Redditor. “No shooting though!”

While these players will be heading out into Leonida to find birds, others will be looking to find the best weapons and cars.

#11113. Artificial neurons successfully communicate with living brain cells

Engineers at Northwestern University have created printed artificial neurons that go beyond imitation and can directly interact with real brain cells. These flexible, low-cost devices produce electrical signals that closely resemble those generated by living neurons, allowing them to activate biological brain tissue.

In experiments using slices of mouse brain, the artificial neurons successfully triggered responses in real neurons. This result shows a new level of compatibility between electronic devices and living neural systems.

Toward Brain Interfaces and Energy-Efficient AI

This advance moves researchers closer to electronics that can directly interface with the nervous system. Potential uses include brain-machine interfaces and neuroprosthetics, such as implants that could help restore hearing, vision, or movement.

The technology also points toward a new generation of computing systems inspired by the brain. By replicating how neurons communicate, future hardware could perform complex tasks using far less energy. The brain remains the most energy-efficient computing system known, and scientists hope to apply its principles to modern technology.

The study will be published on April 15 in the journal Nature Nanotechnology.

"The world we live in today is dominated by artificial intelligence (AI)," said Northwestern's Mark C. Hersam, who led the study. "The way you make AI smarter is by training it on more and more data. This data-intensive training leads to a massive power-consumption problem. Therefore, we have to come up with more efficient hardware to handle big data and AI. Because the brain is five orders of magnitude more energy efficient than a digital computer, it makes sense to look to the brain for inspiration for next-generation computing."

Hersam is an expert in brain-inspired computing and holds multiple roles at Northwestern University, including the Walter P. Murphy Professor of Materials Science and Engineering at the McCormick School of Engineering. He also is a professor of medicine at Northwestern University Feinberg School of Medicine and a professor of chemistry at the Weinberg College of Arts and Sciences. In addition, he serves as chair of the department of materials science and engineering, director of the Materials Research Science and Engineering Center, and a member of the International Institute for Nanotechnology. He co-led the study with Vinod K. Sangwan, a research associate professor at McCormick.

Why the Brain Outperforms Traditional Silicon

Modern computers handle increasing workloads by packing billions of identical transistors onto rigid, two-dimensional silicon chips. Each component behaves the same way, and once manufactured, the system remains fixed.

The brain works very differently. It consists of many types of neurons, each with specialized roles, arranged in soft, three-dimensional networks. These networks are constantly changing, forming and adjusting connections as learning occurs.

"Silicon achieves complexity by having billions of identical devices," Hersam said. "Everything is the same, rigid and fixed once it's fabricated. The brain is the opposite. It's heterogeneous, dynamic and three-dimensional. To move in that direction, we need new materials and new ways to build electronics."

Although artificial neurons have been developed before, most produce overly simple signals. To achieve more complex behavior, engineers typically need large networks of devices, which increases energy use.

Printable Materials Enable Brain-Like Behavior

To better replicate real neural activity, Hersam's team built artificial neurons using soft, printable materials that more closely match the brain's structure. Their approach relies on electronic inks made from nanoscale flakes of molybdenum disulfide (MoS2), which acts as a semiconductor, and graphene, which serves as an electrical conductor. These materials were deposited onto flexible polymer surfaces using aerosol jet printing.

Previously, researchers treated the polymer in these inks as a flaw because it interfered with electrical performance. As a result, they removed it after printing. In this work, the team used that same feature to enhance the device.

"Instead of fully removing the polymer, we partially decompose it," he said. "Then, when we pass current through the device, we drive further decomposition of the polymer. This decomposition occurs in a spatially inhomogeneous manner, leading to formation of a conductive filament, such that all the current is constricted into a narrow region in space."

That narrow conductive path produces a sudden electrical response similar to a neuron firing. The resulting device can generate a wide variety of signals, including single spikes, continuous firing, and bursting patterns, closely resembling real neural communication.

Because each artificial neuron can produce more complex signals, fewer components are needed to perform advanced tasks. This could significantly improve computing efficiency.

Testing Artificial Neurons on Real Brain Tissue

To evaluate whether the artificial neurons could truly interact with living systems, the researchers partnered with Indira M. Raman, the Bill and Gayle Cook Professor of Neurobiology at Weinberg. Her team applied the artificial signals to slices of mouse cerebellum.

The results showed that the electrical spikes matched key biological properties, including their timing and duration. These signals reliably activated real neurons and triggered neural circuits in a way similar to natural brain activity.

"Other labs have tried to make artificial neurons with organic materials, and they spiked too slowly," Hersam said. "Or they used metal oxides, which are too fast. We are within a temporal range that was not previously demonstrated for artificial neurons. You can see the living neurons respond to our artificial neuron. So, we've demonstrated signals that are not only the right timescale but also the right spike shape to interact directly with living neurons."

Low-Cost, Sustainable Manufacturing and AI Implications

Beyond performance, the new approach offers environmental and practical advantages. The manufacturing process is simple and inexpensive, and the additive printing method places material only where it is needed, reducing waste.

Improving energy efficiency is especially important as artificial intelligence systems grow more demanding. Large data centers already consume vast amounts of power and require significant water for cooling.

"To meet the energy demands of AI, tech companies are building gigawatt data centers powered by dedicated nuclear power plants," Hersam said. "It is evident that this massive power consumption will limit further scaling of computing since it's hard to imagine a next-generation data center requiring 100 nuclear power plants. The other issue is that when you're dissipating gigawatts of power, there's a lot of heat. Because data centers are cooled with water, AI is putting severe stress on the water supply. However you look at it, we need to come up with more energy-efficient hardware for AI."

The study, "Multi-order complexity spiking neurons enabled by printed MoS2 memristive nanosheet networks," was supported by the National Science Foundation.

#11111. DJ Khaled is giving Xbox players doctor’s notes so they can skip work for GTA 6

Xbox has teamed up with DJ Khaled to give gamers an excuse to skip work and spend the day playing video games, complete with personalized doctor’s notes.

The rapper and producer has taken on the role of “Dr. XBOX” as part of a new promotional campaign encouraging players to take time off during a major week for gaming.

Announced on November 16, the campaign allows fans to generate their own doctor’s notes online, although Xbox has made it clear that the documents aren’t medically certified and won’t necessarily convince employers to approve a day off.

Players interested in getting a personalized doctor’s note can visit the website, where Xbox has launched a dedicated website for the promotion.

The notes are presented as prescriptions from DJ Khaled, who claims to have discovered a widespread condition called “Vitamin G deficiency” after observing symptoms including controller cramps, late-night gaming dreams, and excitement surrounding new releases.

“After extensive research (and a whole lot of gaming), I’ve come to one conclusion: there’s a serious case of Vitamin G deficiency around the world. And I’m here to tell you that the cure is simple,” Khaled explained.

The musician encourages players to use their personalized notes however they like, whether that means displaying them at home, sharing them with friends, or showing them to their bosses.

“Your very own prescription for more gaming. Put it on your fridge. Show your boss. Send it to your group chat. Let the people know that Dr. XBOX has spoken,” he said.

However, the promotion comes with a disclaimer confirming that the notes have no medical validity.

#11107. AI swarms could hijack democracy without anyone noticing

A new kind of political threat may be emerging, and it is far less visible than protests or traditional voter manipulation. Researchers warn that highly realistic AI-controlled personas could soon play a major role in shaping public opinion and influencing democratic systems.

A recent policy forum paper published in Science describes how large groups of AI-generated personas can convincingly imitate human behavior online. These systems can enter digital communities, participate in discussions, and influence viewpoints at extraordinary speed. Unlike earlier bot networks, these AI agents can coordinate instantly, respond to feedback, and maintain consistent narratives across thousands of accounts.

Rapid progress in large language models and multi-agent systems has made it possible for a single operator to manage vast networks of AI "voices." These personas can appear authentic, adopt local language and tone, and interact in ways that feel natural to other users.

They are also capable of running millions of small-scale experiments to determine which messages are most persuasive. This allows them to refine their communication strategies in real time and generate what appears to be widespread public agreement. In reality, that consensus is artificially created and designed to influence political discussions.

Deepfakes and fake news signal early risks

Although fully developed AI swarms are still largely theoretical, researchers say there are already warning signs. These include AI-generated deepfakes and fake news outlets that have influenced recent election conversations in countries such as the United States, Taiwan, Indonesia, and India, according to UBC computer scientist Dr. Kevin Leyton-Brown.

At the same time, monitoring organizations have identified pro-Kremlin networks spreading large volumes of online content. This activity is believed to be aimed at shaping the data used to train future AI systems, potentially influencing how those systems behave and what information they prioritize.

Experts warn of growing impact on democracy

Looking ahead, experts believe AI swarms could significantly affect the balance of power in democratic societies. Dr. Leyton-Brown cautioned that these systems are likely to change how people trust information online. "We shouldn't imagine that society will remain unchanged as these systems emerge. A likely result is decreased trust of unknown voices on social media, which could empower celebrities and make it harder for grassroots messages to break through."

Researchers suggest that upcoming elections may serve as a critical test for this technology. The key challenge will be recognizing and responding to these AI-driven influence campaigns before they become too widespread to control.

#11105. Brandon Buckingham cancels all video trips over fears for his life as health worsens

YouTuber Brandon Buckingham has canceled all upcoming filming trips and collaborations after revealing his health is deteriorating, warning that continuing to push himself could cost him his life.

The content creator, known for his documentary-style videos and interviews, has been battling serious medical problems since 2025, when he was hospitalized with multiple organ failure.

Although Buckingham returned to making YouTube videos earlier this year, he revealed in a November 16 Instagram Story that his condition has worsened and he has been hospitalized again.

“I regret to inform you I have cancelled all video trips and collaborations for the foreseeable future,” he wrote.

“My health is bad and getting worse. I fear if I continue pushing myself I will lose my life.”

Buckingham went on to detail the frightening symptoms he is experiencing, including purple hands, swollen feet, and persistent vomiting.

“I was just hospitalized AGAIN. My hands are purple, my feet are swollen, I am constantly vomiting. I can’t let this go on any longer,” he explained.

Despite the concerning update, the YouTuber insisted he is taking steps to improve his condition and remains determined to recover.

“But don’t worry, I am being very proactive, and I WILL bounce back,” he added.

The announcement comes just days after Buckingham uploaded a new documentary filmed in India on November 12, titled India’s City of Trash | Inside the Most Polluted Town on Earth.

The video follows Buckingham and fellow creator Turkey Tom as they explore life around a massive landfill in Delhi.

TikToker FunBucketKustoms says he briefly died & “can’t walk anymore” after head-on moped crash

YouTuber Brandon Buckingham shares recovery update after life-threatening illness

Brandon Buckingham pleads for help after almost dying amid heart failure diagnosis

Buckingham’s latest health scare comes nearly a year after he was rushed to intensive care with multiple organ failure in November 2025.

At the time, he revealed that his heart, lungs, liver, and kidneys were failing, with doctors treating him for sepsis, pneumonia, and other serious complications.

His condition prompted an outpouring of support, with MrBeast and thousands of fans helping raise more than $300,000 toward his medical expenses.

In January 2026, Buckingham shared an encouraging recovery update, revealing that his liver, kidneys, and pancreas had recovered. However, he said he was still experiencing heart failure and permanent lung damage.

Buckingham has not disclosed the cause of his latest deterioration or indicated when he expects to resume filming.

#11104. Top 4 MCP Servers for Marketers: Features, Use Cases, and Limitations

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

You no longer need to build a dashboard every time you want to investigate a drop in traffic or compare campaign results. With servers built on the Model Context Protocol (MCP), you can ask an AI assistant a question and let it retrieve the relevant data directly from your marketing tools.

But connecting an assistant to Google Analytics is not the same as connecting it to HubSpot or Ahrefs. Each server exposes different data, capabilities and levels of access. Some are built for quick analysis inside one platform, while others support reporting across several channels. This guide compares four servers, what they can do, how they are set up and where their limits matter. Details such as plans and tool lists change often, so confirm them against each vendor's documentation before you commit.

The summary below reflects each vendor's documentation at the time of writing.

Google publishes an official, open-source Google Analytics MCP server for GA4. It connects AI assistants to the Admin and Data APIs, so it can list accounts and properties, run standard, realtime, funnel and conversion reports, and inspect custom dimensions and metrics. That makes it useful for investigating traffic changes, landing page performance and engagement trends.

Example prompt: "Which landing pages lost the most engaged sessions this month compared with last month?" The assistant can pull the relevant reports and help you spot changes, and you can then check whether the decline comes from specific channels, countries or devices.

Setup: The server runs locally. Google's documentation lists Python 3.10 or later, pipx or uvx, a Google Cloud project with the Analytics Admin and Data APIs enabled, application default credentials with the read-only Analytics scope, and at least Viewer access on the property you want to query.

Access: Google describes the server as available for read requests only, so it cannot change your Analytics configuration.

Limitations: It covers GA4 only, so it cannot combine website metrics with CRM records or advertising costs on its own. Running it locally also means you maintain the configuration yourself.

Best for: Web analysts and technical marketers who regularly investigate GA4 performance.

Marketing performance usually spans several platforms. Google Ads generates clicks, GA4 tracks site activity and a CRM records leads. Seeing the whole picture means bringing those metrics together.

Coupler is a no-code data integration platform that offers MCP access to prepared business data. It lists more than 400 supported data sources, including Google Ads, Facebook Ads, GA4, HubSpot, LinkedIn Ads and Shopify, and lets teams combine and transform data before an assistant queries it.

Example prompt: "What was our cost per lead across Google Ads, Meta Ads and LinkedIn last quarter?" For paid search specifically, Coulper describes a Google Ads MCP server that lets you ask about campaigns and compare them with other connected channels.

Setup: Coupler is hosted, so there is nothing to install locally. You connect your platforms, configure data flows, choose refresh schedules and connect a compatible AI assistant. According to the vendor, queries run against the prepared datasets, which is useful for recurring reports because teams can reuse refreshed data instead of preparing exports by hand.

Best for: Agencies and teams that need consistent reporting across advertising, website analytics, ecommerce and CRM platforms.

HubSpot's remote MCP server became generally available on April 13, 2026. It connects compatible AI clients to CRM data such as contacts, companies, deals, tickets and activities, and can also read marketing objects such as campaigns, landing pages and blog posts. That helps marketers investigate lead quality, pipeline performance and lifecycle trends without reviewing records one by one.

Example prompt: "Which deals created last quarter originated from webinar leads, and how many closed?" The assistant can retrieve the records and examine conversion patterns, provided the attribution data exists.

Setup: HubSpot hosts the server. In your account you create an MCP auth app under Development, then connect an MCP client that supports OAuth 2.1 with PKCE to the HubSpot endpoint. See HubSpot's remote MCP server documentation for current steps.

Access: Unlike a purely analytical server, HubSpot supports selected create and update actions on records such as contacts, companies, deals, tickets and activities. Other objects, including marketing content, are read-only.

Limitations: Available operations differ by object type, and incomplete lead sources or inconsistent lifecycle stages will weaken any analysis.

Best for: B2B marketers and revenue operations teams that manage customers and pipeline in HubSpot.

Ahrefs offers a hosted MCP server that brings its SEO data into compatible assistants. It supports conversational research into keywords, backlinks, competing pages and organic visibility, so you can follow up with questions instead of exporting report after report.

Example prompt: "Which websites link to both of our main competitors but don't link to us?" The assistant can retrieve backlink data and help you identify outreach targets, which you can then narrow by Domain Rating or organic traffic.

Setup: According to the Ahrefs MCP documentation, the remote server is available on paid plans starting from Lite, and you authorize it through your AI client. Ahrefs also publishes a getting started guide.

Limitations: Calls made through MCP use your monthly API units in the same way as direct API usage, and rows per request depend on your plan. Ahrefs documents a default cap of 200,000 units per month for keys created through OAuth, which workspace admins can change. The data is also SEO-focused rather than first-party conversion analytics.

Best for: SEO specialists, content marketers and link builders who already use Ahrefs.

Yes, but start with the connections your team actually needs. Each additional server adds tools the assistant has to choose between, which increases complexity. Combining Ahrefs and HubSpot, for example, supports separate SEO and CRM investigations, but connecting several servers does not automatically create a reliable cross-platform reporting system.

For recurring reports, prepare consistent datasets and validate the first results against your existing dashboards. That catches errors caused by mismatched metrics or attribution rules.

Are MCP servers safe for marketing data? Security depends on the provider, the authentication method and the permissions you grant. Use trusted servers, review access scopes, and enable write operations only when necessary.

Do I need coding skills to use MCP servers? Not necessarily. Hosted services usually need less configuration, while local servers such as Google's need extra setup.

Which AI clients support MCP? Claude, ChatGPT, Cursor and other tools support MCP connections, though functionality and setup differ.

Do MCP servers cost extra? It depends on the provider. Some require paid plans, and others consume API credits or have usage limits.

Pick the server that matches the question you ask most often: Google Analytics MCP for GA4 behavior, Coupler for cross-channel reporting, HubSpot for CRM and lifecycle analysis, and Ahrefs for SEO research. Start with read-only access, check usage limits and permissions, and validate early answers against the reports you already trust.

#11100. This AI knew the answers but didn’t understand the questions

Psychologists have long debated whether the human mind can be explained by a single, unified theory or if different functions such as attention and memory must be studied separately. Now, artificial intelligence (AI) is entering that debate, offering a new way to explore how the mind works.

In July 2025, a study published in Nature introduced an AI model called "Centaur." Built on standard large language models and refined using data from psychological experiments, Centaur was designed to simulate human cognitive behavior. It reportedly performed well across 160 tasks, including decision-making, executive control, and other mental processes. The results drew widespread attention and were seen as a possible step toward AI systems that could replicate human thinking more broadly.

A more recent study published in National Science Open challenges those claims. Researchers from Zhejiang University argue that Centaur's apparent success may come from overfitting. In other words, instead of understanding the tasks, the model may have learned to recognize patterns in the training data and reproduce expected answers.

To test this idea, the researchers created several new evaluation scenarios. In one example, they replaced the original multiple-choice prompts, which described specific psychological tasks, with the instruction "Please choose option A." If the model truly understood the task, it should have consistently selected option A. Instead, Centaur continued to choose the "correct answers" from the original dataset.

This behavior suggests that the model was not interpreting the meaning of the questions. Rather, it relied on learned statistical patterns to "guess" answers. The researchers compared this to a student who scores well by memorizing test formats without actually understanding the material.

The findings highlight the need for caution when assessing the abilities of large language models. While these systems can be highly effective at fitting data, their "black-box" nature makes it difficult to know how they arrive at their outputs. This can lead to issues such as hallucinations or misinterpretations. Careful and varied testing is essential to determine whether a model truly has the skills it appears to demonstrate.

The Real Challenge: Language Understanding

Although Centaur was presented as a model capable of simulating cognition, its biggest limitation appears to be in language comprehension. Specifically, it struggles to recognize and respond to the intent behind questions. The study suggests that achieving true language understanding may be one of the most important challenges in developing AI systems that can model human cognition more fully.

#11099. 9-year-old sparks 48-hour snake hunt after fooling firefighters with AI python in toilet

A nine-year-old boy in Italy sparked a 48-hour search for a missing python after using AI to create a fake image of a snake emerging from a toilet.

The incident took place in Castelfidardo, near Ancona, Italy, on November 12, when the youngster showed adults a convincing image of what appeared to be a royal python poking its head out of a toilet bowl.

Believing the snake was real, the adults raised the alarm at around 8pm, prompting firefighters from the nearby town of Osimo to respond.

However, after searching the bathroom and the rest of the property, emergency crews were unable to find any trace of the reptile.

As reported by Need To Know, firefighters initially suspected the snake may have escaped through an open window or hidden itself somewhere inside the home.

The search continued the following day, with specialist officers from the Carabinieri Forestali’s CITES unit, which handles cases involving protected and exotic species, joining the investigation.

It was only after officers examined the original photograph on the boy’s phone that they discovered the truth.

The nine-year-old admitted he had used an AI app to digitally insert the python into a photograph of the toilet.

He had been visiting a friend when he created the image in the bathroom before showing it to the adults and claiming he had spotted a snake.

After realizing how much trouble his prank had caused, the boy reportedly apologized to the officers.

Despite the emergency response and subsequent investigation, he will face no legal consequences, as children under 14 cannot be held criminally responsible under Italian law.

#11095. Businessman arrested after booby-trapped game console explodes at rival’s electronics store

According to g1, the suspect allegedly hired a delivery driver through an app to transport the console to the store under the pretense that it needed to be modified to play games.

The driver, who was reportedly unaware that he was transporting a bomb, delivered the console at approximately 5:30 PM.

Security footage captured the terrifying moment the device detonated, damaging the ceiling and furniture while leaving two people injured, including the store’s owner.

One victim could be seen with his clothing on fire, while another limped away holding his injured leg.

Police believe the device was deliberately designed to kill, with forensic examinations revealing that it contained a timer and two cylinders filled with pressurized flammable liquid.

Investigators suspect the attack was motivated by business competition, as Lima reportedly owns three electronics stores, including two on the same street where the victim had just opened his business.

#11094. AI lets chemists design molecules by simply describing them

Creating new molecules is one of the toughest tasks in chemistry. Whether the goal is a life-saving drug or a cutting-edge material, each compound must be built through a carefully planned series of reactions. Mapping out these steps requires deep expertise and strategic thinking, which is why chemists often spend years mastering the process.

A major hurdle is retrosynthesis. In this approach, chemists begin with the final molecule they want and work backward to figure out simpler starting materials and possible reaction routes. This involves many decisions, such as selecting the right building blocks, deciding when to form rings, and determining whether sensitive parts of the molecule need protection. While computers can scan enormous "chemical spaces," they still struggle to match the strategic judgment of experienced chemists.

Another challenge involves reaction mechanisms, which describe how reactions proceed step by step through the movement of electrons. Understanding these mechanisms allows scientists to predict new reactions, improve efficiency, and avoid costly trial and error. Although current computational tools can suggest many possible pathways, they often lack the intuition needed to pinpoint the most realistic ones.

Researchers led by Philippe Schwaller at EPFL have developed a new method that uses large language models (LLMs) as reasoning tools for chemistry. Rather than directly generating chemical structures, these models act as evaluators that guide existing computational systems.

The new framework, called Synthegy, combines traditional search algorithms with AI that can interpret chemical strategies written in natural language.

"When making tools for chemists, the user interface matters a lot, and previous tools relied on cumbersome filters and rules," says Andres M Bran, the first author of the Synthegy paper published in Matter. "With Synthegy, we're giving chemists the power to just talk, allowing them to iterate much faster and navigate more complex synthetic ideas."

How Synthegy Improves Retrosynthesis Planning

Synthegy starts with a target molecule and a simple instruction written in everyday language. For example, a chemist might request that a specific ring be formed early or that unnecessary protecting groups be avoided. Standard retrosynthesis software then generates many possible pathways.

Each of these pathways is converted into text and reviewed by a language model. Synthegy scores how well each option matches the chemist's instructions and explains its reasoning. This makes it easier to rank and filter the best routes. By guiding searches with natural language, chemists can quickly focus on strategies that align with their goals.

Understanding Reaction Mechanisms With AI

Synthegy applies a similar method to reaction mechanisms. It breaks reactions down into basic electron movements and explores different possibilities. The language model evaluates each step and steers the search toward pathways that make chemical sense.

The system can also incorporate additional details, such as reaction conditions or expert hypotheses, provided as text. This flexibility allows researchers to refine their analysis and explore more realistic scenarios.

In synthesis planning, Synthgey was able to identify pathways that matched complex strategic instructions. In a double-blind study, 36 chemists provided 368 valid evaluations, and their assessments agreed with the system's results 71.2% of the time on average.

The framework can flag unnecessary protecting steps, judge how feasible reactions are, and prioritize efficient solutions. It also demonstrates that LLMs can operate at multiple levels, from analyzing functional groups to evaluating entire synthetic routes. Larger models performed best, while smaller ones showed more limited abilities.

This research highlights a different way AI can support chemistry. Instead of replacing human decision-making, Synthegy positions language models as guides that help interpret and refine computational results. Chemists can describe their goals in plain language and receive solutions that reflect their strategy.

The approach could speed up drug discovery, improve reaction design, and make advanced tools more accessible to scientists.

"The connection between synthesis planning and mechanisms is very exciting: we usually use mechanisms to discover new reactions that enable us to synthesize new molecules," says Andres M Bran. "Our work is bridging that gap computationally through a unified natural language interface."

#11091. How Developers Can Improve Their Learning and Productivity With AI

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Artificial intelligence (AI) is changing how developers learn, write code, and manage their daily work. Tasks that once required hours of searching, testing, and manual effort can now be completed with help from AI. Developers can use it to understand complex programming concepts, find coding errors, learn new technologies, and complete repetitive tasks.

However, AI is not only useful for writing code faster. It can also help developers improve their technical knowledge, solve problems, and build better learning habits. When used correctly, it can become a useful assistant throughout the software development process.

The key is to understand where AI can help and where human skills are still needed. Here are several practical ways developers can use AI to improve their learning and productivity.

Learning a new programming language or understanding a difficult concept can take time. Developers often need to read long explanations, watch tutorials, and test different examples before they understand how something works.

AI can make this process easier by explaining technical concepts in simple language. Instead of searching through several websites, developers can ask direct questions and receive explanations based on their current knowledge.

For example, a developer learning JavaScript can ask AI to explain promises, callbacks, or asynchronous functions. It can break these topics into smaller parts and provide simple code examples to make them easier to understand.

Another useful approach is to ask AI to explain the same concept in different ways. If one explanation is difficult to understand, a different example or comparison may make the idea clearer.

AI explanations are not always accurate, especially when dealing with complex programming concepts or unfamiliar technologies. Developers should verify important information before using it in their projects. Hallucination detection can help identify fabricated facts, incorrect technical explanations, and misleading information in AI-generated responses, making it easier to check the accuracy of what they learn.

This helps developers spend less time struggling with unfamiliar topics and more time building their technical knowledge.

Software development changes regularly. New frameworks, libraries, programming languages, and development methods continue to appear. Developers need to keep learning if they want to stay up to date.

AI can support this process by helping developers create learning plans based on their goals and current skills.

For example, a developer who wants to learn Python can ask AI to create a beginner-friendly learning plan. The plan can include basic syntax, variables, loops, functions, object-oriented programming, and small projects.

AI can also recommend practical exercises for each topic. Instead of only reading about a programming concept, developers can write code and test their understanding.

Here are some ways to use AI when learning new technologies:

However, developers should not depend entirely on AI-generated explanations. They should also read official documentation and test examples themselves. This helps them understand how technologies work in real development environments.

AI can help developers improve their coding skills by providing examples, explaining different solutions, and identifying areas that need improvement.

For example, a developer may write a function that works but contains unnecessary steps. They can ask AI to review the code and suggest ways to make it easier to read, maintain, or understand.

AI can also explain why one solution may be better than another. This allows developers to learn different approaches instead of always following the first solution they find.

Some practical ways to use AI for coding improvement include:

Developers can also ask AI to create small coding challenges based on their skill level. For example, someone learning Python can request exercises about lists, dictionaries, loops, and functions.

The important thing is to understand the code instead of copying it without review. Developers who examine the logic behind each solution can improve their programming knowledge over time.

Developers learn new information from many sources, including online courses, technical blogs, documentation, tutorials, and coding projects. Without a proper system, it can become difficult to find useful information when they need it again.

AI can help developers organize this knowledge and make learning resources easier to review.

For example, developers can use AI to summarize long technical explanations, create revision questions, and organize important programming concepts into smaller topics. They can also turn their learning notes into structured study guides that make it easier to revisit important information.

A well-organized knowledge system can be useful when learning a new framework or preparing for technical interviews. Instead of searching through old browser tabs or reading the same documentation repeatedly, developers can return to their saved explanations and notes.

Here are some useful ways to organize technical knowledge:

Technical documentation can contain detailed explanations that are difficult to remember. Developers can use AI to summarize important sections and turn them into short notes.

For example, when learning a new API, they can save information about its main functions, parameters, common errors, and practical examples.

Reading information does not always mean remembering it. Developers can ask AI to create questions from their notes so they can test their understanding.

For example, after learning about database indexing, they can create questions about how indexes work, when to use them, and how they affect query performance.

Developers should also save important information about their projects. This may include coding decisions, API details, database structures, and solutions to common problems.

Keeping these details organized makes it easier to return to a project after a break or help another developer understand the work.

Developers spend a large part of their working time on tasks that do not always require complex problem-solving. These may include writing basic code structures, creating test cases, preparing documentation, and handling repeated coding patterns.

AI can help reduce this manual effort by generating suggestions and automating parts of these tasks.

For example, when starting a new project, developers often need to create similar code structures, such as basic functions, configuration files, and reusable components. An AI coding assistant can generate an initial version that developers can review and adjust.

Automating these activities gives developers more time to focus on important parts of their projects, such as system design, complex features, and problem-solving.

However, generated code should always be checked before it is added to a project. Developers need to make sure it follows project requirements, coding standards, and security rules.

Finding and fixing bugs is an important part of software development. Even experienced developers can spend hours trying to understand why a program is not working as expected.

AI can help make debugging easier by explaining error messages, identifying possible causes, and suggesting solutions.

For example, when a developer encounters a Python error, they can share the relevant code and error message with an AI assistant. The assistant can explain what the error means and suggest possible ways to fix it.

AI can also help developers review code before submitting it. It may identify common issues such as:

Another useful approach is to ask AI to explain why a particular bug occurs instead of only requesting a corrected version of the code.

This helps developers understand the problem and avoid making the same mistake in future projects.

Still, AI suggestions are not always correct. Developers should test proposed fixes, review the surrounding code, and confirm that the solution does not introduce new problems.

Managing several development tasks at once can be difficult. Developers may need to work on new features, fix bugs, review code, attend meetings, and meet project deadlines.

AI can help organize these responsibilities by breaking large tasks into smaller and more manageable steps.

For example, if a developer needs to build a user authentication system, they can ask AI to divide the work into separate tasks. These may include creating registration forms, setting up database tables, implementing login logic, adding password protection, and testing the system.

This makes it easier to understand the work involved and decide what needs attention first.

AI can also help explain technical requirements before development begins. This may reduce confusion and help developers identify missing details early.

However, AI-generated plans should be treated as starting points. Developers still need to consider their actual deadlines, technical limitations, and project requirements before following them.

Although AI offers many benefits, depending on it for every programming task can affect a developer's ability to solve problems independently.

If developers always ask AI to write code, fix errors, and explain every concept, they may miss opportunities to develop their own technical skills.

A 2026 Anthropic study found that participants using AI scored 17 percentage points lower on a programming knowledge quiz than participants who worked without AI in the experiment. This highlights why understanding the work still matters when using coding assistants.

Developers can avoid excessive dependence on AI by following a few simple practices:

When facing a coding problem, spend some time understanding it and trying possible solutions. If you get stuck, use AI to explore the issue rather than asking it to complete everything.

Never add AI-generated code to a project without understanding what it does. Review the logic, check the functions, and test the output.

AI can sometimes provide outdated information, incorrect code, or explanations that do not fit a particular project. Check important details against official documentation and reliable technical resources.

Regular coding practice without assistance helps developers maintain their problem-solving skills. It also shows whether they truly understand a programming concept.

The best approach is to use AI as a helpful assistant while keeping independent thinking and technical judgment at the center of development work.

Using AI occasionally can be helpful, but developers can get more value from it by making it part of a regular learning and working routine.

A simple daily routine can help developers balance their project responsibilities with continuous learning.

For example, developers can follow this approach:

Start with a learning goal: Spend some time understanding a new programming concept, framework, or technology. Use AI to explain difficult topics and provide practical examples.

Plan the day's development tasks: Ask AI to help organize important tasks and break complex work into smaller steps.

Use AI during development: Get help with unfamiliar code, debugging, test cases, and repetitive tasks. Review all suggestions before using them.

Save useful information: Keep important explanations, coding examples, and solutions in an organized knowledge base for future reference.

Review what you learned: At the end of the day, revisit important concepts and test your understanding through small questions or coding exercises.

Developers do not need to spend hours following this routine. Even a short period of focused learning each day can help them build knowledge while managing their regular development work.

AI is becoming a useful part of modern software development. It can help developers understand difficult concepts, learn new technologies, improve code quality, debug problems, and complete repetitive tasks more efficiently.

However, better productivity does not simply mean writing more code in less time. Developers also need to understand what they are building, improve their problem-solving skills, and keep learning as technology changes.

The most effective approach is to use AI for support rather than letting it make every decision. By combining AI assistance with regular practice, independent thinking, and organized learning, developers can improve both their technical skills and everyday productivity.

#11087. Your “um” and pauses could reveal early dementia risk

The way people speak during ordinary conversations could offer valuable insight into brain health, according to new research from Baycrest, the University of Toronto, and York University. Scientists found that subtle speech characteristics, including pauses, filler words such as ('uh,' 'um'), and difficulty retrieving words, are closely connected to executive function, the group of mental abilities involved in memory, planning, attention, and flexible thinking.

The findings provide some of the strongest evidence so far linking natural speech patterns with key cognitive abilities. The work also expands on earlier research showing that older adults who speak more quickly tend to maintain stronger thinking skills over time (Wei et al., 2024).

"The message is clear: speech timing is more than just a matter of style, it's a sensitive indicator of brain health," says Dr. Jed Meltzer, Senior Scientist at Baycrest's Rotman Research Institute and senior author on this study, titled "Natural Speech Analysis Can Reveal Individual Differences in Executive Function Across the Adult Lifespan."

AI Analysis Reveals Hidden Cognitive Clues

For the study, participants were shown detailed images and asked to describe them in their own words. They also completed established tests designed to measure executive function.

Researchers then used artificial intelligence to examine the speech recordings in depth. The AI system detected hundreds of subtle speech features, including the length and frequency of pauses, the use of filler words, and timing-related patterns in speech. These markers consistently predicted how well participants performed on cognitive tests, even after researchers adjusted for factors such as age, sex, and education.

Executive function naturally weakens with age and is often affected during the early stages of dementia. However, standard cognitive testing can be difficult to repeat frequently because it takes time and people often improve simply from becoming familiar with the tests.

Natural speech may offer a simpler alternative. Because speaking is part of daily life, it can be measured repeatedly and unobtrusively on a large scale. Researchers also noted that speech provides valuable insight into processing speed and overall cognitive function in real-world situations, without requiring strict time limits that are common in many traditional cognitive assessments.

The team believes speech analysis could eventually become a practical way to identify people whose cognitive decline is progressing faster than expected and who may face a higher risk of developing dementia.

"This research sets the stage for exciting opportunities to develop tools that could help track cognitive changes in clinics or even at home. Early detection is critical for any cure or intervention, as dementia involves progressive degeneration of the brain that may be slowed," says Dr. Meltzer.

Future Research on Brain Health Monitoring

The researchers say more long-term studies are needed to follow changes in speech over time and distinguish normal aging from the earliest signs of disease. They also suggest that combining speech analysis with other health measures could make early detection of cognitive decline more accurate, practical, and widely available.

This research was supported by the Mitacs Accelerate program and the Natural Sciences and Engineering Research Council of Canada (NSERC).

#11086. Xbox shuts down confusion over whether Project Helix is actually a PC

Microsoft has clarified what gamers can expect from its next-generation Xbox console, Project Helix, addressing questions about whether the upcoming hardware will essentially be a gaming PC.

With Xbox increasingly bringing its games and services to PC, handhelds, and other platforms, the distinction between a traditional console and a Windows gaming device has become less clear.

Project Helix aims to bridge that gap by supporting PC games while retaining the experience players expect from Xbox hardware.

In a new article published by Xbox, Jason Ronald, the company’s Vice President of Next Generation, explained how Microsoft is approaching the system’s development and why it isn’t simply building a gaming PC with Xbox branding.

Speaking about the system’s design philosophy, Ronald made it clear that Microsoft still considers Project Helix a dedicated gaming console, despite its expanded PC capabilities.

“We’re very clear on the design goal: Helix is designed to be a console that is also capable of playing games from your PC,” he said.

“It is not a PC that can also play console games. The goal is making it easy, intuitive, and instant to access my games. But if you’re a hardcore gamer or a power user who wants to have that deeper level of customizability across settings and performance options, you can absolutely go do it.”

Microsoft is designing Project Helix to offer the simplicity of a traditional console while giving players access to PC games, removing the need to manage operating system updates, graphics drivers, and other technical settings that can complicate the experience.

Xbox CEO describes next-gen Helix console as a “family of devices”

Next-gen Xbox consoles could reportedly cost up to $1,800

Next Xbox console will play every generation according to leaked Microsoft memo

Ronald explained that the hardware should allow players to jump directly into their games without worrying about operating system updates, graphics drivers, or complicated settings.

“With this, I think about what makes a console magical, it’s that it just makes my games instantly accessible and ready to play in an instant. I don’t have to worry about updating my operating system, updating drivers, configuring different settings – things like that. The hardware and the experience is entirely designed around playing games.”

However, Microsoft isn’t abandoning the flexibility of PC gaming entirely. Players who want to customize performance settings and other technical options will still have access to those features.

The company also pointed to the ROG Xbox Ally and its dedicated Xbox mode as important steps toward Project Helix. Feedback from the handheld’s integration of PC and console game libraries has helped shape Microsoft’s plans for its next-generation interface.

Ronald described the handheld as a precursor to Project Helix, explaining that its development has provided valuable insight into how players interact with games across different platforms.

Beyond PC compatibility, Microsoft is emphasizing game preservation and backward compatibility as major priorities for its next console.

Ronald said Project Helix is intended to bring together Xbox’s existing library while allowing players to carry their games and progress between devices.

“When I think about Project Helix,” explains Jason Ronald, XBOX Vice President of Next Generation, “it’s really designed to be the ultimate gaming machine – one that removes all the barriers of play that have historically existed. My entire library is accessible to me. I’m able to bring four generations of my XBOX library and progression with me and have it available instantly. And we remove the arbitrary constraints between console and PC games.”

Microsoft also wants to make moving between devices a more seamless experience, with Ronald describing a future where players can resume games regardless of which screen they’re using.

“It’s also this notion that not only does my library move with me, but my progress moves with me as well. Because I fully expect that when I’m in the middle of an 80-hour RPG, if I’m 25 hours into that and I want to play it on another device, I should be able to instantly resume from where I was.”

The company says backward compatibility remains a core part of its strategy, building on preservation efforts introduced with the Xbox One and expanded through the Xbox Series X|S generation.

Microsoft is also looking beyond traditional hardware improvements such as higher resolutions and frame rates. Ronald highlighted neural rendering as one area of development that could improve visual fidelity without requiring proportionally greater computational power.

“We have to invent entirely new technology to continue to advance the state-of-the-art,” he explained, pointing to the rising costs of hardware components as another challenge facing the next generation.

Project Helix is still in development, and Microsoft has not confirmed its final specifications, pricing, release date, or which PC storefronts will be supported.

Although Project Helix will support PC games and offer additional customization options for more experienced users, Microsoft is maintaining its focus on delivering a traditional console experience while expanding the range of games players can access.

#11083. New quantum algorithm solves “impossible” materials problem in seconds

Quantum computers and other advanced quantum technologies rely on specialized quantum materials that behave in unusual ways under the right conditions. In some cases, scientists can even create entirely new quantum properties by carefully changing a material's structure. One striking example involves stacking sheets of graphene and twisting them into a moiré pattern, which can suddenly turn the material into a superconductor.

Researchers can arrange these layers into even more complicated structures, including quasicrystals and super-moiré materials. But predicting how these exotic materials will behave is extraordinarily difficult. Quasicrystals are so mathematically complex that simulating them can involve more than a quadrillion numbers, a scale far beyond the reach of today's most powerful supercomputers.

Quantum Algorithm Solves Massive Materials Problem

Scientists at Aalto University's Department of Applied Physics have now developed a quantum-inspired algorithm capable of handling these enormous non-periodic quantum materials almost instantly. Assistant Professor Jose Lado says the work also highlights a promising feedback cycle within quantum technology itself.

"Crucially, these new quantum algorithms can enable the development of new quantum materials to build new paradigms of quantum computers, creating a productive two-way feedback loop between quantum materials and quantum computers," he explains.

The advance could eventually support the development of dissipationless electronics, which conduct electricity without energy loss. Such systems may help reduce the growing heat and energy demands of AI-driven data centers.

The research team was led by Lado and included doctoral researcher Tiago Antão, who served as the paper's main author; QDOC doctoral researcher Yitao Sun; and Academy Research Fellow Adolfo Fumega. Their findings were recently published in Physical Review Letters as an Editor's Suggestion.

The researchers focused on topological quasicrystals, unusual materials that host unconventional quantum excitations. These excitations are especially valuable because they help protect electrical conductivity from disruptive noise and interference. However, they are distributed unevenly throughout the already highly complex structure of a quasicrystal.

Rather than attempting to directly calculate the full structure of the material, the team reformulated the challenge using methods similar to those used by quantum computers.

"Quantum computers work in exponentially large computational spaces, so we used a special family of algorithms to encode those spaces, known as tensor networks, to compute a quasicrystal with over 268 million sites. Our algorithm shows how colossal problems in quantum materials can be directly solved with the exponential speed-up that comes from encoding the problem as a quantum many-body system," Antão says.

At this stage, the work remains theoretical and was carried out through simulations, but researchers say experimental testing and future applications are already coming into view.

"The quantum-inspired algorithm we demonstrated enables us to create super-moiré quasicrystals several orders of magnitude above the capabilities of conventional methods. That is an instrumental step towards designing topological qubits with super-moiré materials for use in quantum computers, for example," Lado says.

Toward Practical Quantum Computing Applications

According to Lado, the algorithm could eventually be adapted to operate on actual quantum computers once the hardware becomes sufficiently advanced.

"Our method can be adapted to run on real quantum computers, once they reach necessary scale and fidelity. In particular, the new AaltoQ20 and the Finnish Quantum Computing Infrastructure can play a significant role for future demonstrations," Lado says.

The findings suggest that studying and designing exotic quantum materials may become one of the earliest practical applications for quantum algorithms and quantum computing systems.

The project also brings together two major areas of Finnish quantum research: quantum materials and quantum algorithms. It is part of Lado's ERC Consolidator grant ULTRATWISTROICS, which focuses on designing topological qubits using van der Waals materials, as well as the Center of Excellence in Quantum Materials QMAT, whose goal is to advance the quantum technologies of the future.

Materials provided by Aalto University. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#11081. Professor cancels homework and exams during GTA 6 launch week

A professor in Spain cleared his class’s calendar for GTA 6‘s launch week, and the email explaining why quickly went viral.

The gesture landed while grown-ups were already plotting their own escapes from the hotly anticipated release, with one economist predicting 1.5 million Americans could skip work on November 19.

Brands piled in, too, as Xbox enlisted DJ Khaled to hand out doctor’s notes for launch day and KFC lined up a fake funeral as an excuse to stay home.

His students, however, had someone in their corner who’d spent the week before quietly clearing their schedule for launch.

In an email written in Spanish and shared on X on November 16, the professor told his students they wouldn’t have any homework, projects or deadlines in his subject during GTA 6’s launch week.

On top of that, he pushed an exam back from the 23rd to the 30th “so you can organize yourselves better and have more free time during those days.”

En españa, un profesor le suspendio todas las tareas y evaluaciones a los alumnos para que puedan disfrutar el GTA 6Tiene mas ganas de jugar el que todos los pibes jsjs pic.twitter.com/YgvACvursu

“When GTA V came out in 2013, I was your age,” he explained, recalling how badly he’d wanted to get home to play and how it was all he and his friends talked about.

The surprise came with a catch, though. He apologized for the heavier workload the week before, explaining he had kept the class toiling precisely so they could get ahead on the syllabus, and warned they’d have to buckle down again once launch week was over.

He also urged them to “enjoy responsibly,” adding that some things in the game weren’t quite appropriate for their age, with GTA 6’s age ratings already flagging sex, drugs and graphic violence.

“I had my moment with GTA V, and I was excited for you to have yours with GTA VI,” he wrote near the end. The email couldn’t be independently verified, though it spread quickly after accounts including content creator ElBuni shared it.

He isn’t the only one clearing the decks with the launch barreling toward November 19, either, after a car company announced back in June that it would pause operations for the day so its entire staff could play.

#11077. How Motion Design Improves SaaS Onboarding and Feature Adoption

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Most SaaS products don't lose users because the software is bad. They lose users in the first few minutes, before anyone has seen what the product can actually do. Someone signs up, lands on an empty dashboard, feels a flicker of confusion, and quietly leaves. The industry has a name for the gap between signing up and getting value: activation. Closing it is one of the hardest problems in product design.

Motion design is one of the most underused tools for closing that gap. Used well, animation isn't decoration sprinkled on at the end. It's a layer of communication that tells users where to look, confirms that their actions worked, and makes a complex interface feel learnable instead of overwhelming. This article looks at why motion influences behaviour during onboarding, the specific patterns that move the needle on activation and feature adoption, and how to build them responsibly as a developer — with an eye on performance and accessibility throughout.

Motion works in onboarding because it speaks to how human perception actually operates, not because it looks modern. A few principles do most of the heavy lifting.

It directs attention. The eye is drawn to movement before it reads a single word. A gently pulsing button or an element that slides into view tells a new user where to go next without a paragraph of instructions. On a crowded first-run screen, that guidance is the difference between a confident first action and a confused exit.

It preserves context. When a panel expands, a card flips, or a new view slides in from the right, a short transition shows users where things came from and where they went. This spatial continuity keeps people oriented. Interfaces that snap instantly between states force the user to re-scan the screen each time and silently rebuild their mental model.

It confirms actions. Every click, toggle, and form submission is a small question: did that work? A quick visual response — a checkmark drawing itself, a row settling into place — answers it immediately. Without that feedback, users repeat actions, doubt the system, or assume something broke.

It shapes perceived performance. A thoughtful loading animation or skeleton screen makes a wait feel shorter and the product feel responsive, even when the underlying request takes exactly as long. Perception of speed is part of the experience, and motion is how you manage it.

These are the patterns that reliably help new users reach their first win.

Onboarding doesn't end after the first session. Most SaaS products keep shipping features, and each one faces its own adoption problem: users settle into a few familiar workflows and never discover the rest. Motion is how you reintroduce the product to people who already think they know it.

Contextual nudges that appear in motion — a tooltip that eases in near a newly relevant button, a gentle highlight on a feature the moment a user hits the limit it solves — are far more effective than a generic email. They arrive at the point of need and use movement to pull attention without hijacking the screen.

"What's new" reveals can announce an update with a small, well-timed animation rather than a modal that gets dismissed on reflex. A badge that animates in, or a panel that slides open on the first visit to a page, respects the user's flow while still being noticed.

Animated demonstrations carry the most weight for genuinely complex features. Showing a feature in motion — rather than describing it in text — lets users grasp a multi-step workflow in seconds. For intricate capabilities, this is where a short, purpose-made explainer earns its place, a point we return to in the next section.

The right tool depends on how complex the animation is and where it lives. Roughly in order of weight:

CSS transitions and keyframe animations should be your default. They cover the large majority of onboarding motion — hovers, state changes, highlights, progress bars — and the browser can run them efficiently. Reach for JavaScript only when CSS genuinely can't express what you need.

The Web Animations API gives you CSS-level performance with programmatic control, which matters when animations depend on data or user input, or need to be sequenced and interrupted. SitePoint's guide to the Web Animations API is a solid introduction, and the broader web animation archive covers libraries like GSAP for orchestrating complex timelines.

Lottie is the bridge between designer-made animation and the web. Designers export vector animations as JSON, and a lightweight player renders them natively at small file sizes — ideal for polished illustrative moments like success states and animated empty states, without hand-coding every frame.

Pre-rendered video is the right call when a feature is genuinely involved and a looping CSS animation or a tooltip simply can't carry the explanation. A short, well-produced walkthrough can compress a multi-step workflow into something a user absorbs in under a minute, which is why many teams lean on a dedicated SaaS explainer video for their most complex features and onboarding moments. The trade-off is weight and production effort, so reserve video for the handful of places where it genuinely outperforms in-interface motion, lazy-load it, and never autoplay it with sound.

Motion that stutters does more damage than no motion at all, and motion that ignores accessibility can actively harm users. Two disciplines keep animation on the right side of that line.

For performance, animate the properties the browser can handle cheaply: transform and opacity. These can run on the compositor thread without triggering layout or paint, which is what lets animation hold a smooth 60fps. Animating properties like width, height, top, or left forces the browser to recalculate layout on every frame and is the usual cause of janky motion. Keep animations short — most interface transitions should land between 150 and 300 milliseconds — because motion that overstays its welcome makes a product feel slow.

For accessibility, honour the prefers-reduced-motion media query. Some users experience dizziness, nausea, or disorientation from movement, and the operating system lets them ask for less of it. Respecting that setting is straightforward:

Beyond that, never let an animation block interaction. If a user has to wait for a flourish to finish before they can click, the motion is working against them. Animation should support the task and then get out of the way.

Motion design is a product decision, so treat it like one and measure it. The metrics that matter map directly to the problems motion is meant to solve.

Treat animations as hypotheses and A/B test them. Ship the animated onboarding step to half of your new users and measure activation against the control. Motion should earn its place with data, not assumptions — and occasionally the data will tell you an animation is slowing people down, which is just as useful to know.

The best onboarding motion is the kind users never consciously notice. It simply makes the product feel clear, responsive, and easy to learn — guiding attention, confirming actions, and turning a complex interface into something approachable. That's the real payoff: not animation for its own sake, but fewer confused users, faster time to value, and features that actually get discovered.

Start small. Pick the single point in your onboarding where users most often stall, add one purposeful animation that removes the confusion, and measure what happens. Motion, used deliberately, is one of the most direct levers you have on activation and adoption — and like any good tool, it works best when it draws no attention to itself.

#11076. NASA’s new AI space chip could let spacecraft think for themselves

NASA is developing a powerful new computer chip designed to dramatically increase the intelligence and performance of future spacecraft. Through a commercial partnership, the project is creating advanced processing technology capable of helping spacecraft operate more independently during missions far from Earth.

NASA's High Performance Spaceflight Computing project is focused on boosting the computing capabilities of spacecraft used in space exploration. Current missions rely on older processors because they are durable enough to survive the extreme conditions of space. While those chips are dependable, they lack the performance needed for more advanced missions.

The agency says newer and far more capable processors are essential for future autonomous spacecraft, faster onboard scientific analysis, and supporting astronauts during missions to the Moon and Mars.

"Building on the legacy of previous space processors, this new multicore system is fault-tolerant, flexible, and extremely high-performing," said Eugene Schwanbeck, program element manager in NASA's Game Changing Development program at the agency's Langley Research Center, in Hampton, Virginia. "NASA's commitment to advancing spaceflight computing is a triumph of technical achievement and collaboration."

Radiation Hardened Processor Faces Extreme Testing

At the center of the project is a new radiation-hardened processor built to deliver up to 100 times the computing power of today's spaceflight computers while surviving the harsh environment of space. Engineers at NASA's Jet Propulsion Laboratory (JPL) in Southern California are running a wide range of tests designed to simulate those conditions.

"We are putting these new chips through the wringer by carrying out radiation, thermal, and shock tests while also evaluating their performance through a rigorous functional test campaign," said Jim Butler, High Performance Space Computing project manager at JPL.

To qualify for spaceflight, the processor must withstand intense electromagnetic radiation and dramatic temperature fluctuations that can damage electronics. High-energy particles from the Sun and deep space can also trigger computer errors that force spacecraft into "safe mode," temporarily shutting down nonessential systems until engineers resolve the issue.

NASA is also testing how the chip handles the challenges of planetary landings.

"To simulate real-world performance, we are using high-fidelity landing scenarios from real NASA missions that would typically require power-intensive hardware to process huge volumes of landing-sensor data," said Butler. "This is an exciting time for us to be working on hardware that will enable NASA's next giant leaps."

Testing at JPL began in February and is expected to continue for several months. Early results have been highly encouraging. According to NASA, the processor is functioning as intended and has shown performance levels roughly 500 times greater than the radiation-hardened chips currently used in spacecraft.

The team also marked the beginning of testing with a symbolic moment by sending an email titled "Hello Universe," referencing the famous introductory messages used during the early days of computer programming.

AI Powered Spacecraft and Deep Space Missions

The processor is being developed jointly by JPL and Microchip Technology Inc., based in Chandler, Arizona. The company is working with NASA through a commercial partnership, and sample chips have already been shared with defense and commercial aerospace partners.

The technology is expected to play a major role in the future of autonomous spacecraft. With onboard artificial intelligence, spacecraft could respond to unexpected situations in real time when communication delays make human control impractical. The chip could also help deep space missions process, store, and transmit massive amounts of scientific data back to Earth more efficiently.

NASA says the processor may eventually support crewed missions to the Moon and Mars as well.

Small Processor With Massive Computing Power

The device is known as a system-on-a-chip (or SoC), meaning it combines the essential components of a computer into a single compact unit. The processor includes central processing units, computational offloads, advanced networking systems, memory, and input/output interfaces.

SoCs are widely used in smartphones and tablets because they are compact and energy efficient. However, NASA's version is designed to survive for years in deep space, potentially traveling millions (or even billions) of miles from Earth without maintenance or repairs.

Once the processor is certified for use in space, NASA plans to integrate it into a wide variety of missions, including Earth orbiters, planetary rovers, deep space probes, and crewed habitats.

The technology could also have benefits on Earth. Microchip plans to adapt the processor for industries such as aviation and automotive manufacturing.

The project is managed by the Space Technology Mission Directorate's Game Changing Development (GCD) program at NASA Langley. The GCD program and JPL, which is managed by Caltech in Pasadena, California, oversaw the development process from mission planning and industry studies through final delivery.

NASA JPL selected Microchip as a partner in 2022, and the company funded its own research and development work on the processor.

Materials provided by NASA's Jet Propulsion Laboratory. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.
