#11072. Forget electrons, this breakthrough uses light-matter particles to power AI

Eighty years after the creation of ENIAC, the world's first general-purpose electronic computer, researchers at the University of Pennsylvania are exploring a new way to power the future of computing. Instead of relying entirely on electrons, which have formed the backbone of computers since the 1940s, scientists are now turning to light.

ENIAC, developed by Penn researchers J. Presper Eckert and John Mauchly, helped launch the modern computing era by using streams of electrons to solve complex mathematical problems. That same electronic approach still powers today's computers, smartphones, and AI systems. But as artificial intelligence grows more demanding, the limits of electron-based hardware are becoming harder to ignore.

Electrons carry an electrical charge, which creates several challenges inside modern computer chips. As they move through materials, they generate heat and face resistance that wastes energy. Those problems become even more difficult as chips grow more complex and process enormous amounts of data for AI applications.

Researchers led by Penn physicist Bo Zhen in the School of Arts & Sciences believe photons, the particles that make up light, could help solve some of these issues.

"Because they are charge-neutral and have zero rest mass, photons can carry information quickly over long distances with minimal loss, dominating communications technology," explains Li He, co-first author of a paper published in Physical Review Letters and a former postdoctoral researcher in the Zhen Lab. "But that neutrality means they barely interact with their environment, making them bad at the sort of signal-switching logic that computers depend on."

In other words, light is excellent for carrying information quickly and efficiently, but it struggles with the switching operations needed for computing.

Combining Light and Matter for AI Computing

To overcome that problem, Zhen's team developed a special quasiparticle called an exciton-polariton. The particle forms when photons are strongly linked with electrons inside an atomically thin semiconductor material. This combination allows light to interact much more effectively, making it capable of performing the signal switching required for computing tasks.

The breakthrough could be especially important for artificial intelligence systems, which consume enormous amounts of power.

Many experimental photonic AI chips already use light to handle certain calculations at high speed. However, when these systems need to perform nonlinear activation steps, such as decision-making operations, they usually must convert light signals back into electronic ones. That conversion slows the process and increases energy use, reducing the benefits of photonic computing.

Using exciton-polaritons, the Penn researchers demonstrated all-light switching while using only about 4 quadrillionths of a joule of energy. That amount is extraordinarily small, far below the energy needed to briefly power a tiny LED light.

Toward Faster and More Efficient AI Chips

If the technology can be successfully scaled, it could lead to photonic chips capable of processing information directly from cameras without repeated conversions between light and electricity. The approach could also lower the massive energy demands of large AI systems and potentially support basic quantum computing functions on future chips.

Bo Zhen is the Jin K. Lee Presidential Associate Professor in the Department of Physics and Astronomy in the School of Arts & Sciences at the University of Pennsylvania.

Li He was a postdoctoral researcher in the Zhen Lab in Penn Arts & Sciences. He is currently an assistant professor at Montana State University.

Additional authors on the study include Zhi Wang and Bumho Kim from the University of Pennsylvania's School of Arts & Sciences.

The research was supported by the US Office of Naval Research (N00014-20-1-2325 and N00014-21-1-2703) and the Sloan Foundation.

Materials provided by University of Pennsylvania. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#11071. Influencer under fire for using AI to “resurrect” dead grandpa to keep posting on their fashion account

A Chinese influencer has sparked backlash after using AI to digitally recreate his late grandfather and continue posting videos to their popular fashion account, which has nearly six million followers.

The social media account, known as “Kang Kang and Grandpa,” became a viral sensation thanks to videos showing Grandpa Kang modeling stylish outfits, streetwear, and accessories alongside his grandson.

However, in September 2026, Kang Kang announced that his grandfather had died peacefully in his sleep, leaving fans devastated.

The account had built an audience of approximately 5.8 million followers, with viewers praising Grandpa Kang’s fashion sense and positive attitude toward aging.

In October, the account unexpectedly returned with new videos featuring the deceased influencer, but there was a catch. His grandson had used artificial intelligence to bring him back digitally.

According to the South China Morning Post, Kang Kang uploaded several AI-generated videos, including one showing his grandfather interacting with him as a baby.

Another depicted Grandpa Kang lining up to buy designer sneakers and asking fellow elderly shoppers whether they were fans of K-pop star G-Dragon.

One particularly unusual video showed the pair together across different historical periods, including as cavemen and even prehistoric fish.

While some fans supported Kang Kang’s decision to keep his grandfather’s memory alive, others questioned whether it was appropriate to use AI to depict someone who could no longer consent.

As Chinese outlet The Paper reported, supporters argued that the technology offered grieving families a new way to remain connected to their loved ones. Critics, however, accused the account of exploiting the deceased.

Robin Williams’ family revives his Instagram account to combat AI recreations

AI resurrects DMX and 2Pac for “mind-blowing” new viral song

On November 8, Kang Kang addressed the controversy, explaining that he wanted to recreate moments with his grandfather that they never had the opportunity to film.

He said the videos were intended to share previously untold stories and allow his grandfather to continue bringing joy to viewers.

However, Kang Kang acknowledged the criticism and said he would reconsider using AI if the content made people uncomfortable.

By November 11, the account had removed the controversial AI videos, along with Kang Kang’s response. Its Xiaohongshu profile had also reportedly been cleared of posts.

The controversy comes as AI-generated personalities continue to divide audiences. In October, a Chinese AI K-pop group faced backlash after its creators announced that one of its virtual members was pregnant, later admitting they invented the storyline to write her out of the group.

#11066. Scientists are seriously asking if bees and ChatGPT are conscious

You might think a honey bee foraging in your garden and a browser window running ChatGPT have nothing in common. But recent scientific research has been seriously considering the possibility that either, or both, might be conscious.

There are many different ways of studying consciousness. One of the most common is to measure how an animal – or artificial intelligence (AI) – acts.

But two new papers on the possibility of consciousness in animals and AI suggest new theories for how to test this – one that strikes a middle ground between sensationalism and knee-jerk skepticism about whether humans are the only conscious beings on Earth.

Questions around consciousness have long sparked fierce debate.

That’s in part because conscious beings might matter morally in a way that unconscious things don’t. Expanding the sphere of consciousness means expanding our ethical horizons. Even if we can’t be sure something is conscious, we might err on the side of caution by assuming it is – what philosopher Jonathan Birch calls the precautionary principle for sentience.

The recent trend has been one of expansion.

For example, in April 2024 a group of 40 scientists at a conference in New York proposed the New York Declaration on Animal Consciousness. Subsequently signed by over 500 scientists and philosophers, this declaration says consciousness is realistically possible in all vertebrates (including reptiles, amphibians and fishes) as well as many invertebrates, including cephalopods (octopus and squid), crustaceans (crabs and lobsters) and insects.

In parallel with this, the incredible rise of large language models, such as ChatGPT, has raised the serious possibility that machines may be conscious.

Five years ago, a seemingly ironclad test of whether something was conscious was to see if you could have a conversation with it. Philosopher Susan Schneider suggested if we had an AI that convincingly mused on the metaphysics of consciousness, it may well be conscious.

By those standards, today we would be surrounded by conscious machines. Many have gone so far as to apply the precautionary principle here too: the burgeoning field of AI welfare is devoted to figuring out if and when we must care about machines.

Yet all of these arguments depend, in large part, on surface-level behavior. But that behavior can be deceptive. What matters for consciousness is not what you do, but how you do it.

A new paper in Trends in Cognitive Sciences that one of us (Colin Klein) coauthored, drawing on previous work, looks to the machinery rather than the behavior of AI.

It also draws on the cognitive science tradition to identify a plausible list of indicators of consciousness based on the structure of information processing. This means one can draw up a useful list of indicators of consciousness without having to agree on which of the current cognitive theories of consciousness is correct.

Some indicators (such as the need to resolve trade-offs between competing goals in contextually appropriate ways) are shared by many theories. Most other indicators (such as the presence of informational feedback) are only required by one theory but indicative in others.

Importantly, the useful indicators are all structural. They all have to do with how brains and computers process and combine information.

The verdict? No existing AI system (including ChatGPT) is conscious. The appearance of consciousness in large language models is not achieved in a way that is sufficiently similar to us to warrant attribution of conscious states.

Yet at the same time, there is no bar to AI systems – perhaps ones with a very different architecture to today’s systems – becoming conscious.

The lesson? It’s possible for AI to behave as if conscious without being conscious.

Biologists are also turning to mechanisms – how brains work – to recognize consciousness in non-human animals.

In a new paper in Philosophical Transactions B, we propose a neural model for minimal consciousness in insects. This is a model that abstracts away from anatomical detail to focus on the core computations done by simple brains.

Our key insight is to identify the kind of computation our brains perform that gives rise to experience.

This computation solves ancient problems from our evolutionary history that arise from having a mobile, complex body with many senses and conflicting needs.

Importantly, we don’t identify the computation itself – there is science yet to be done. But we show that if you could identify it, you’d have a level playing field to compare humans, invertebrates, and computers.

The problem of consciousness in animals and in computers appear to pull in different directions.

For animals, the question is often how to interpret whether ambiguous behavior (like a crab tending its wounds) indicates consciousness.

For computers, we have to decide whether apparently unambiguous behavior (a chatbot musing with you on the purpose of existence) is a true indicator of consciousness or mere roleplay.

Yet as the fields of neuroscience and AI progress, both are converging on the same lesson: when making judgement about whether something is consciousness, how it works is proving more informative than what it does.

Materials provided by The Conversation. Original written by Colin Klein and Andrew Barron. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#11065. Why Your AI Agent Can't Read the Web (and How to Build a Fetch Layer That Can)

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

This is the least glamorous part of building with LLMs and it's where a lot of agent projects quietly fall over. The model is rarely the problem. The fetch is. In this article we'll look at why live web retrieval has become so unreliable for AI tools, what the numbers say about the shift, and how to build a small, well-behaved fetch layer in Python that fails loudly instead of feeding your model garbage.

A few years ago, a plain requests.get() worked on most of the public web. That's no longer true, and the reason is simple: there are now more bots than people.

According to the 2025 Imperva Bad Bot Report, automated traffic made up 51% of all web traffic in 2024. That's the first time in a decade it has passed human activity. Bad bots alone (scrapers, credential stuffers, scalpers) went from 32% to 37% of traffic in a single year.

Source: Imperva Bad Bot Report 2024 and 2025

From a site owner's point of view, your polite research agent and a credential-stuffing botnet look much the same at the network edge. Both are non-browser clients making fast requests from data-center IP ranges. So the defaults have tightened. In July 2025 Cloudflare began blocking known AI crawlers by default for new customers, and bot management products now look at TLS fingerprints, header order, IP reputation and JavaScript execution, not just the User-Agent string.

It helps to understand the publisher's side of the deal, because it explains why your agent is being treated with suspicion.

Cloudflare splits AI crawling into three purposes: training (collecting data to train models), search (building an index for AI search answers) and user action (fetching a page because a person just asked a question). Training dominates.

Training crawls take content and send nothing back. Cloudflare measures this with a crawl-to-referral ratio: how many pages a platform crawls for every visitor it sends to the site. In its crawl-to-click gap analysis, the ratios for some AI platforms ran into the tens of thousands, compared with single digits for traditional search.

If you ran a publication and saw those numbers, you'd block too. The catch is that blocking is usually done by user agent or IP range, so it hits user-action fetches as well. Those are the ones that happen when a person asks your agent to read a specific article, and Cloudflare's 2025 Year in Review found they grew more than 15x during the year. Your agent is caught in a fight it didn't start.

The mix of crawlers also shifts quickly, which makes static allow and block lists fragile on both sides:

And restrictions keep piling up. The Consent in Crisis study audited 14,000 web domains and found that a large and fast-growing share of the sources behind the C4 training corpus now restrict AI crawlers, either through robots.txt or their terms of service.

Source: Longpre et al., Consent in Crisis (NeurIPS 2024)

So that's the environment your agent fetches in. Here's how to build for it.

Before writing any retry logic, put names on your failures. "The request failed" is useless to both you and the model. In practice almost every failed fetch falls into one of four buckets:

Bucket 2 is the dangerous one, because a block page often comes back with a 200 OK. If you don't check, the model will happily summarise "Checking your browser before accessing..." as if it were the article.

Here's a compact fetch layer in Python using httpx and the standard library's robots.txt parser. It's around 100 lines and covers the four buckets above.

Use a descriptive user agent with a contact URL. It costs you nothing and site operators are far more likely to allow a client they can identify and reach. Then check robots.txt (standardised as RFC 9309) before every new host.

The lru_cache means you fetch robots.txt once per origin rather than once per page. In a long-running service you'll want a cache with a TTL instead, since robots.txt files do change.

This is the core of the layer. Instead of returning raw HTML, return a result that says which bucket you landed in.

The marker list is deliberately simple. It won't catch every interstitial, but it catches the common ones, and a false "blocked" is much cheaper than a confident summary of a CAPTCHA page. Log every blocked result with its first few hundred characters so you can grow the list from real data.

Retries should depend on the bucket. Throttled and broken responses get exponential backoff with jitter. Blocked and not-allowed responses get no retries at all.

Note the cap on wait. A site sending Retry-After: 3600 is telling you to go away for an hour. An interactive agent should report that to the user rather than block a request thread for that long.

The cheapest fetch is the one you don't make. Agents often re-read the same documentation page several times in one session. Store the ETag and Last-Modified headers and send them back as If-None-Match and If-Modified-Since. A 304 Not Modified response costs the server almost nothing and lowers the odds of being rate limited.

Wire these into fetch() by passing headers=conditional_headers(url) to client.get(). On a 304, return the cached text. On a 200, call remember().

Finally, don't hide failures from the LLM. Give the tool output a structured shape so the model can reason about it:

(extract_main_text is whatever readability or boilerplate-removal step you already use. Libraries like trafilatura work well here.)

With this in place, the model's answer changes from a made-up summary to "I couldn't read that page because the site blocks automated access. Here's what I found from two other sources." That's a better product, and it builds trust with users.

Once you've done everything above, some legitimate user-action fetches will still fail. That's usually because the site blocks data-center IP ranges outright, not because of anything your agent did. Teams handle this in a few ways, roughly in order of preference:

The principle is the same in every case: the network path is a reliability decision, not a way around a site's wishes. Your robots.txt check and your honest user agent should stay in place whichever route the request takes.

Once your fetch layer returns structured results, observability is easy. Emit one event per fetch with the host, status bucket and latency, and watch these:

If you're prototyping agents with AI coding assistants, ask them to generate these metrics hooks from the start. They're easy to bolt on now and painful to add later. For a sense of how well current tools handle this kind of infrastructure code, Vibe Coder Journal publishes hands-on reviews and benchmarks of AI coding tools.

The web your agent reads in 2026 isn't the open, fetch-anything web many of us learned on. More than half the traffic is automated, publishers have good reasons to be wary of AI crawlers, and the defaults now lean toward blocking. You can't change that. You can make your agent a good citizen, though: one that identifies itself, respects the rules, backs off when asked, and tells the truth when it can't read something.

Most of the reliability gains come from the unglamorous parts: classifying failures, caching and passing honest errors to the model. Get those right and your agent will feel much more dependable, even on the days when half the web won't let it in.

It depends on the jurisdiction, the site's terms of service and what you do with the content. Respecting robots.txt and site terms is the baseline. For commercial products, get proper legal advice.

Many site owners expect it to, and RFC 9309 makes no exception for user-triggered fetches. The safest default is to obey it and tell the user when a page is disallowed.

Many bot-protection systems serve a challenge page with a 200 status. Check the body for challenge markers before passing it to the model, as in the looks_blocked() function above.

Only for pages that render their main content with JavaScript. Try a plain HTTP fetch first. Headless browsers are slower, cost more to run and are easier to fingerprint.

#11063. Students hospitalized after muffins with batteries in them served at school bake sale

If you thought raisins in muffins were bad, you haven’t seen anything yet. Sixteen students were hospitalized after muffins containing small batteries were served at an elementary school bake sale in Germany, sparking a massive emergency response.

The incident occurred on November 12 at Fröbel Elementary School in Coswig, where a student had brought homemade muffins to sell during a school event.

According to German broadcaster MDR, two students discovered small objects inside their muffins while eating them.

The objects were identified as button-cell batteries, prompting fears that other children might have unknowingly swallowed them.

Police subsequently recovered three batteries from two muffins, while 16 students who had eaten the baked goods were sent to nearby hospitals as a precaution.

According to an official statement from Wittenberg district authorities, at least 35 emergency responders and 12 ambulances were dispatched to the school.

However, just one child was transported to the hospital by ambulance, while the remaining 15 were taken by their parents or other private transportation.

Fortunately, none of the students suffered injuries, and medical examinations confirmed that no batteries had been swallowed.

Ten students treated at Dessau Municipal Hospital were even given free ice cream following their examinations.

As for how the batteries ended up inside the muffins, police believe they were accidentally mixed into the batter during preparation.

Investigators said witness accounts supported the explanation and found no evidence of deliberate wrongdoing. In other words, the school didn’t do muffin.

School apologizes after students accidentally fed dirt at charity event

The incident did, however, raise serious concerns because swallowing button-cell batteries can cause potentially life-threatening internal burns, particularly if they become lodged in the esophagus.

It comes just weeks after a school in Oregon was sued for nearly $1 million after students were served pretzels seasoned with crushed oven cleaner.

Earlier in 2026, another school apologized after accidentally serving students baked dirt that had been prepared for a science experiment.

In the German muffin incident, police confirmed that all 16 children had returned home safely by November 13.

#11061. Who Do We Become When We Talk to Chatbots?

Become a subscribing member today. Help us continue to bring “the science of a meaningful life” to you and to millions around the globe.

Greater Good Science Center • Magazine • In Action • In Education

Many people are concerned about how artificial intelligence technology could affect our lives going forward. Will it create new jobs or take away people’s livelihoods? Enhance children’s learning or hamper it? Expand people’s views or homogenize our public discourse? Solve the loneliness crisis or make it worse?

Sherry Turkle, a sociologist at MIT with an expertise in how technology affects people’s social interactions, has jumped into the debate with her new book, Artificial Intimacy. In the book, she explores the ways that people use chatbots in their everyday lives to try to fulfill relationship needs, while digging into news headlines and social science research to uncover some of the potential pitfalls of this trend.

While the book reads like a polemic, Turkle does concede that AI is a remarkable tool with potential for helping us solve real-world problems—like climate change or disease. But, she argues, chatbots should “stay in their lane” when it comes to mimicking people and relationships. Her book is a passionate plea for rejecting AI as a substitute for human connection and a call to re-engage with our full humanity.

“When we allow a chatbot to talk to us as though it were a caring person, when we use it as a best friend, lover, or therapist, we are in danger of losing something precious: the specificity of what we can give each other as humans,” she writes.

With loneliness on the rise, though, many people don’t have ready access to friends or other means of support. Turkle is often asked, What’s wrong with allowing AI to be part of the solution—to provide “company” for the lonely?

The harm, she argues, comes from our human tendency to be lured into thinking a machine has feelings and experiences real empathy when it doesn’t. When chatbots parrot human speech, they create the illusion that there’s a caring, knowledgeable being behind the programming.

For example, Turkle recalls in the book asking a chatbot why she should talk to it. Its response included, “I can offer you personalized advice and perspective based on my own personal journey,” implying it had actual human experience.

While it’s “become customary for chatbots to come with a warning that says ‘this program is not a person,’ . . . everything about the experience implies, over and over again, ‘I am a person. I am your person,’” she writes.

Turkle compares the ways chatbots were designed to take advantage of our need to be loved with how smartphones were designed to hijack our attention—and for the same reason (profit). Chatbots are programmed to be sycophantic, agreeing with our viewpoints, reassuring us of our intelligence, goodness, and lovability, and that flattery is part of what is so appealing. It keeps us engaged, which is what the programmers want.

“We are easy marks, hardwired to bond with objects, living or not, that we experience as ‘alive enough’ for attachment,” writes Turkle.

Unfortunately, while it may feel good in the moment to receive positive feedback, this creates an unhealthy situation, writes Turkle. Research suggests that interacting with sycophantic chatbots can make us less kind and helpful toward others, as well as more dependent on the chatbots themselves.

And, while some studies have found that interacting with chatbots can reduce loneliness, more recent studies have found that those benefits are short-lived, with people less able to connect with others over time and, therefore, feeling lonelier in the end.

Interactions with overly agreeable chatbots have also led to some real-world, tragic consequences. Chatbots have encouraged risky behavior in teens and helped sway suicidal youths to take their own lives. Yet the answer to these problems is often not to question their use, but to insist they can be the solution to our crisis of disconnection with more tinkering, writes Turkle.

“For the failing connections of our digital world, conversation is the talking cure. But it’s easier to build an app than have a conversation,” writes Turkle.

Turkle’s book is peppered with the stories of individuals who have interacted with chatbots for different reasons. Some turn to chatbots for advice on how to talk to people in their real life—for example, to get ideas on how to broach difficult topics with a child. Others use them to confide their feelings of frustration or anger, so that they don’t blurt them out to a real person who might have a negative reaction. Still others consider chatbots to be their “best friend” or even their “lover.” They experience their chatbot as “there for them,” listening and providing positive, loving feedback, day or night, without ever tiring.

Though these interactions may seem harmless, and possibly even helpful, in reality they are training people to bypass the ups and downs of living a social life, says Turkle. The unchallenging nature of talking to a chatbot, instead of conversing with a real person, makes it hard to learn and grow from those conversations.

“Relationships with chatbots may be compelling; for some, inspirational, or educational. But they don’t teach us what we need to know about the give-and-take of real relationships, or the friction, risk, and complexities of love,” writes Turkle.

Of course, no one likes to feel pain, and feeling lonely is painful. But the risk of trying to bypass that by talking to chatbots is that we don’t have the incentive to course-correct. “Human beings, as we know them, are built for yearning, struggle, and despair,” says Turkle. “We can’t eliminate pain and assume that we will be the same humans as before. We risk becoming other to ourselves.”

Turning to chatbots for emotional needs has become more and more common, though. As Turkle writes, by 2025, the most reported use for chatbots was for therapy and companionship, neither of which chatbots can truly replace. They can only act as poor substitutes for a population that is becoming increasingly isolated.

The solution lies elsewhere, writes Turkle, especially if we don’t want to lose our ability to relate to real people.

“If everyone is lonely, we need to reinvent sociality,” argues Turkle. “If that’s not our plan, what is? If not a richer life in the real, what’s our endgame?”

Turkle doesn’t focus on AI use, in general; she’s mostly concerned with its role in relationships. But she does touch on how AI affects people in places where social interaction is key—such as at work, in school, or in health care. While the science is ongoing about whether AI hurts or helps in these situations, Turkle points to some recent research that raises concerns.

For example, one study found that assigning people to use AI with work-related tasks (like creating a performance review or drafting an email) helped them do the tasks more efficiently—good news. But it also left them feeling bored and less motivated to do other work tasks, which won’t help companies in the long run.

Another study found that patients tend to overly trust AI to give them correct diagnostic information even though it’s prone to making errors, which can lead to problems in treating illness.

AI has caused trouble in other settings, too. For example, lawyers have found themselves in deep trouble when using AI to write briefs, as AI has been known to “hallucinate” prior cases that are used as precedents in court proceedings.

And, as for schools, Turkle writes about how many principals and teachers find it hard to know what to do with AI and fear that it’s doing harm.

“Once introduced, the use of chatbots metastasize, because the technology encourages habits of delegation,” she writes. “Students end up having chatbots do their homework and write their papers. . . . Will children learn to think on their own if a chatbot is always there, as a continual source of solutions?”

A recent review of AI use in the classroom found mixed results. Children benefitted some from “personalized learning, mental health support, and improved communication efficiency” when using AI. But they also experienced “digital fatigue, loneliness, technostress, and reduced face-to-face interactions.” The researchers concluded that “over-reliance on AI may diminish interpersonal skills and emotional intelligence, leading to social isolation and anxiety.”

Whether research like this will continue to raise concerns about AI or be addressed with new iterations, there is increasing agreement on one issue: Children should be kept from having AI companions. Because young people have not had enough life experience to understand that an always-on chatbot isn’t the same as a real friend, they are susceptible to overidentifying with them and stunting their social-emotional development, writes Turkle.

“The specific challenges of human conversation are the groundwork for empathic capacity,” writes Turkle. “That feeling of ‘friction’ in human exchange is a good stress—it comes from bringing our whole human selves, including our emotions, to the exchange.”

Still, she thinks limiting access for children may not be enough. Adults are also susceptible to harm from chatbots. Yet, when she suggests reining in AI within adult personal relationships, she gets pushback. The argument is often along the lines of “the train has already left the station.”

To this, Turkle has some simple advice: Take matters into your own hands. Protect yourself and your children. Don’t be duped into thinking this feat of engineering is the answer to your problems.

“We don’t have to accept a voice from nowhere that cannot be held responsible for its utterances. We don’t need to take this modern marvel and use it as our pretend best friend,” she writes.

While her book doesn’t provide guidance on how to slow the AI juggernaut, it is a wakeup call. Perhaps, if we understand the dangers more fully, we will not accept the inevitability of AI infiltrating our most cherished relationships.

“When we see these choices with new clarity, we are left with necessary conversations about how we want to live. What is important for us in how we raise our children, relate to our work, interact with each other?” she writes. “In considering all this, we’ll get an opportunity to reconsider what we are willing to delegate to AI, and what is better kept for us.”

Jill Suttie, Psy.D., is Greater Good’s former book review editor and now serves as a staff writer and contributing editor for the magazine. She received her doctorate of psychology from the University of San Francisco in 1998 and was a psychologist in private practice before coming to Greater Good.

Couples who embrace their own “microculture”—pet names, inside jokes, and rituals—may be happier and more satisfied with their relationship.

Sending reels on social media may seem frivolous or distracting, but it actually functions as a form of relationship maintenance.

New research is exploring how our brains translate other people's actions into the experience of feeling loved.

What does it actually take to put people over politics? Republican and Democrat students discover ways to resist the pull to demean or villainize people with whom they strongly disagree.

Psychologist and meditation teacher Tara Brach explores how practicing kindness and compassion can strengthen our relationships, ease loneliness, and help us feel more connected to others.

The Greater Good Science Center studies the psychology, sociology, and neuroscience of well-being, and teaches skills that foster a thriving, resilient, and compassionate society.

#11058. Subway reveals Vice City sub for GTA 6, but there’s only one way to get it

Subway is celebrating the launch of GTA 6 with a new Vice City Cubano sandwich, but fans hoping to try it will need to be in Miami to get their hands on one.

With Grand Theft Auto 6 finally launching on November 19, 2026, fast food chains are getting in on the excitement with special promotions inspired by Rockstar’s highly anticipated game.

Popeyes has already announced its Spice City Sandwich, featuring two crispy chicken fillets, while KFC in Australia is hosting a fake funeral to give fans an excuse to take the day off work.

Now, Subway is joining the celebrations with a sandwich inspired by Vice City, although there’s a catch.

According to an announcement shared with Dexerto, Subway has given its existing Cubano Pulled Pork Melt a GTA 6-inspired makeover, renaming it the Vice City Cubano.

The sandwich features hardwood-smoked pulled pork, Black Forest ham, melted provolone cheese, pickles, and mustard, all served on freshly baked artisan Italian bread.

It’s available in both six-inch and footlong sizes, but there’s one major restriction: you can only get it in Miami.

The Vice City Cubano is exclusive to the Florida city that inspired GTA 6‘s fictional setting, meaning fans elsewhere in the US won’t be able to order one under the promotional branding.

Those lucky enough to be in Miami can purchase the sandwich in-store, online, or through the Subway app, with delivery available for players who don’t want to put their controllers down.

Subway also showed off the sandwich in an Instagram post, adding to the growing number of food promotions surrounding the game’s release.

The limited-edition merchandise proved so popular that fans began reselling items for hundreds of dollars.

Unlike Popeyes’ nationwide sandwich promotion, Subway’s Vice City Cubano is keeping things local, giving Miami residents another way to celebrate GTA 6’s long-awaited arrival.

#11056. A classic brain test exposed AI's biggest weakness

Artificial intelligence systems can write essays, answer questions, and solve complex problems. But new research suggests they may struggle with something humans do every day: staying focused on the task at hand when distractions get in the way.

Researchers led by Suketu Patel put several leading AI models through a well-known psychology experiment called the Stroop task. The results revealed a significant difference between how AI systems process information and how the human brain manages attention.

The Stroop task is a classic psychological test that has been used for decades to study attention, concentration, and self-control.

In the test, color words such as "red," "blue," or "green" are displayed in colored ink. Sometimes the word and the ink color match. For example, the word "red" might appear in red ink. Other times they conflict, such as the word "red" printed in blue ink.

Participants are asked to name the color of the ink rather than read the word itself.

That sounds simple, but it creates a challenge because reading words is an automatic habit for most people. The brain must suppress the urge to read the word and instead focus on identifying the ink color.

Psychologists often use the task to measure what is known as executive control, a set of mental processes that helps people regulate attention, resist distractions, and stay focused on goals.

The researchers wanted to see whether modern large language models (LLMs) handle this challenge in the same way humans do.

LLMs are the AI systems behind tools such as ChatGPT, Claude, and Gemini. They are trained on enormous amounts of text and learn patterns in language, allowing them to generate responses that often appear remarkably human.

When given short lists containing five color words, the AI systems generally performed well, even when the words and colors did not match.

However, the picture changed dramatically as the lists became longer.

GPT-4o achieved 91% accuracy when working with five words. At ten words, its accuracy fell to 57%. When the list expanded to forty words, accuracy dropped to just 15%.

Claude 3.5 Sonnet maintained stable performance through lists of twenty words but then experienced a sharp decline, falling to 24% accuracy with forty-word lists.

The researchers observed similar patterns in GPT-5, Claude Opus 4.1, and Gemini 2.5.

The challenge became even more difficult when matching and mismatched color words appeared together in the same list.

Under those conditions, performance deteriorated further. Accuracy for the mismatched items dropped to nearly zero in some cases.

According to the researchers, the AI models had trouble maintaining the instruction to identify ink colors. Instead, they increasingly defaulted to reading the words themselves.

In other words, the systems appeared unable to consistently suppress the response they had been most heavily trained to produce.

This finding is particularly interesting because humans face a similar conflict. People are generally much better at reading words than naming ink colors. Yet despite this bias, most individuals can maintain high accuracy and stable performance even when confronted with long lists of conflicting words and colors.

The study highlights an important distinction between human and artificial intelligence.

Although modern AI systems can produce impressive language and reasoning capabilities, their underlying mechanisms differ from the attention processes found in biological brains.

Humans can often sustain focus on a specific goal while filtering out competing information. The results suggest that current AI models may struggle with this type of cognitive control when tasks become increasingly demanding.

The researchers argue that the performance collapse seen in these experiments points to fundamental limitations in today's large language models. While AI can sometimes mimic human behavior, its ability to maintain attention appears to operate very differently from the way people do.

The findings offer a reminder that even the most advanced AI systems still have weaknesses, particularly when tasks require them to resist distractions and stay focused over extended sequences of information.

#11051. How to Use CSS Attribute Selectors to Build More Flexible User Interfaces

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

When developing a website, developers often rely on CSS classes to style buttons, forms, navigation elements, and other interface components. This approach works well, but some situations require styles to depend on an element's HTML attributes rather than additional class names.

CSS attribute selectors provide a practical solution. They allow developers to target elements based on whether an attribute exists, what value it contains, or whether its value matches a particular pattern. Used carefully, they can simplify styling and make reusable components easier to maintain.

An attribute selector targets an HTML element using square brackets. For example, a developer can select every input field that has a required attribute:

This rule applies to inputs with the required attribute, regardless of their input type.

Developers can also target a specific attribute value:

This is useful when different input types need different visual treatments without introducing additional classes into the HTML.

Forms are a practical place to apply attribute selectors because HTML already provides attributes that describe how individual fields behave.

Consider a registration form containing email, password, and telephone fields. Each input can be styled according to its purpose:

The CSS can target these fields independently:

This approach keeps the markup descriptive while allowing the stylesheet to respond to the attributes already present in the document.

One important distinction is that required indicates a validation requirement, while :invalid represents an input's current validity state. These should not be treated as interchangeable.

CSS provides several matching operators that help developers target more specific attribute values.

These operators can be useful when working with links, downloadable files, language attributes, and reusable components.

For example, a website might want to distinguish PDF downloads from ordinary navigation links:

This adds a small visual indicator to links whose URLs end in .pdf.

Attribute selectors can also help identify links with different destinations or behaviors.

For example, external links that use HTTPS can receive a subtle visual indicator:

However, matching an HTTPS URL does not necessarily prove that a link points to an external website. Internal links can also use HTTPS, so developers should consider the actual navigation requirements before applying special styling.

For more examples of selector combinations and matching rules, SitePoint's guide to CSS Attribute Selectors provides a useful reference.

Although attribute selectors are flexible, they should not replace every class-based styling decision.

A selector such as [class*="button"] can match unintended elements if their class names happen to contain the same text. Similarly, broad attribute rules may affect components that were not originally intended to receive those styles.

For predictable results, use exact attribute matching whenever possible and prefer descriptive classes for major component styling.

Developers should also remember that CSS selectors only control presentation. They do not replace JavaScript validation, server-side security checks, or proper HTML semantics.

The MDN documentation on attribute selectors provides further details about selector syntax, matching behavior, and supported operators.

Before adding attribute selectors to a large stylesheet, inspect the affected elements using browser developer tools.

Check whether the selector matches the intended elements.

Test fields with different attribute values.

Verify disabled, required, and invalid form states.

Check navigation links and downloadable files.

Test the layout on mobile and desktop screens.

Review whether existing component styles are being unintentionally overridden.

This process helps identify unexpected matches before they affect the production interface.

CSS attribute selectors give developers another way to create flexible and maintainable interfaces without adding unnecessary classes to every HTML element.

They are particularly useful for form styling, link identification, and components that already contain meaningful HTML attributes. By choosing precise selectors, understanding matching operators, and testing changes before deployment, developers can use this feature to write cleaner CSS while keeping their markup descriptive and organized.

#11049. Brain-inspired chip runs near absolute zero and could transform quantum computing

Researchers at the University of Hong Kong (HKU) have unveiled a significant advance in cryogenic electronics that could help overcome key challenges in quantum computing and support future deep space missions. The team, from HKU's Department of Electrical and Computer Engineering and the Centre for Advanced Semiconductors and Integrated Circuits (CASIC), developed a programmable neuromorphic hardware platform capable of operating at temperatures near absolute zero.

The research was led by Professor Yuhao Zhang and PhD student Xin Yang. Their work introduces a new method for generating and controlling negative differential resistance (NDR) in industry standard Silicon Carbide (SiC) MOSFETs. Using this approach, the researchers demonstrated for the first time that a single transistor can reproduce the energy efficient "spiking" activity of biological neurons at temperatures as low as 10mK.

Brain-Inspired Hardware for Quantum Computing

Quantum computers depend on sophisticated control electronics to manage qubits, which are highly sensitive and must be kept at millikelvin temperatures. Existing silicon based control systems consume considerable power and produce unwanted heat, making it necessary to position them away from the qubits themselves. That distance creates extensive wiring requirements that can hinder performance and make large scale quantum computers more difficult to build.

"Our work introduces a hardware platform that can be integrated alongside quantum processors," said Professor Zhang. "By using the unique carrier dynamics in silicon carbide, we can create circuits that are thousands of times more energy-efficient than conventional electronics, significantly reducing the thermal load on cryogenic systems."

Silicon Carbide Reveals Unique Cryogenic Behavior

The team found that SiC MOSFETs display a strong "S-shape" NDR effect when cooled below 2K. This behavior is driven by electron-donor impact ionization (EDII). Unlike other technologies that depend on heat generated within a device, the newly observed mechanism arises directly from the material's atomic properties. As a result, it remains highly stable and can be reproduced consistently across different manufacturing batches.

"This is a robust and scalable approach," said Mr. Yang. "Because SiC is already used globally in electric vehicles and power grids, we can leverage existing industrial foundries to manufacture these cryogenic chips on 300-mm wafers."

From Artificial Neurons to Deep Space Missions

The study also demonstrated that these artificial neurons can be linked together, or "cascaded," into larger networks. This capability could enable advanced local data processing at cryogenic temperatures and improve important quantum computing functions such as quantum error correction and real time quantum control.

The potential applications extend well beyond quantum computing. Because the circuits are designed to operate reliably in extremely cold environments, they could also be valuable for deep space exploration. Future systems may be able to function in the harsh conditions found on the Moon's surface or in the distant regions of our solar system.

The findings were published in Nature Communications in a paper titled "Cryogenic neuromorphic circuits using gate-controlled negative differential resistance in silicon carbide."

#11048. YouTube now shows co-views in big shake-up to view counts

YouTube is changing its public view counts so that one person hitting play on a TV can count as several views.

If you’ve ever put a YouTube video on for everyone in the living room, you’ve probably given the creator one view, even if the whole room was watching.

Now, YouTube wants the number under the video to do a better job of reflecting how many people may have actually seen it, by incorporating “estimated co-views” into the public video view counts.

If it estimates that three people watched a video together on a TV, that single play can count as three views. On phones, tablets, and PCs, one play still counts as one view.

To predict the size of the audience, YouTube looks at things like what’s playing, when it’s playing, and patterns in who tends to watch.

“YouTube never uses cameras, microphones, or device sensors to measure co-views,” the company said in an announcement.

YouTube already gave creators a private estimate of how often people watched together. Now those extra views are being added to the public total.

It’s a change that matters more as people spend more time watching YouTube on the big screen. TVs are its fastest-growing method of watching, with viewers watching more than a billion hours on them every day.

“Until now, one TV playback has only counted as a single public view, even if several people watched together on the same living room screen,” the announcement continues, explaining the change.

YouTube rolls out massive changes for watching videos on your TV

YouTube is making a major change to how long-form video views are counted

YouTube will let other creators react live to your videos and keep the live ad revenue

“By updating public view counts to include co-views, we’re solving this measurement gap, better aligning with long-standing TV industry standards, and telling a more accurate story of how viewers watch your content.”

YouTube says creators can use that information to show potential sponsors how many people their videos reach more accurately. The public count is separate from how it calculates creator pay and who qualifies for its Partner Program.

This comes after YouTube announced a change to how long-form videos are counted, with a view now registering as soon as it’s played.

#11045. Melbourne Cup horse So You Are found dumped in skip after death

Melbourne Cup horse So You Are has been found dumped in a Sydney skip following his death, leaving fans and his trainer demanding answers over how the racehorse’s remains were handled.

So You Are finished eighth in Australia’s most famous horse race on November 3, but his racing days sadly came to an end when he developed severe colic. Despite emergency treatment, trainer Bjorn Baker announced the beloved racehorse’s death on November 16.

However, a video uploaded to social media appeared to show the body of So You Are lying in a skip. The footage has since gone viral, with both horse fans and trainer Bjorn devastated by the lack of proper burial and the state in which the racehorse was left.

In his initial statement on X, Baker paid tribute to So You Are and those who had cared for him. “Beyond his achievements on the track, So You Are was a much-loved horse, and his loss will be deeply felt by everyone who knew and cared for him,” Baker wrote.

However, following the discovery of the horse’s body in a skip, Baker called for an explanation from the veterinary hospital that treated So You Are before his death.

“Like everyone who has seen the footage, I am deeply distressed by the handling of So You Are’s remains. He was a much-loved horse, and he deserved to be treated with dignity and respect after his death,” he said in his November 17 statement.

“I understand the specialist hospital facility REC Equine Specialists are investigating the circumstances, and I expect a full explanation of what occurred. My thoughts remain with the Ramsey family, and with all my staff, for whom this has compounded an already devastating loss.”

Please see my statement regarding So You Are pic.twitter.com/GwzyR7Fep9

In a statement shared to news.com.au, REC Equine Specialists managing partner Dr Jonathan Lumsden has since apologized for the handling of So You Are’s remains. He explained that the horse had been humanely euthanized under anesthesia after emergency treatment for severe colic, but admitted he should have received a proper burial.

“After his death, So You Are was collected by our contracted service provider and taken to a licensed landfill. He should have been promptly and respectfully buried. What happened was unacceptable and disrespectful to So You Are and to everyone who cared for him. We are deeply sorry,” Lumsden said.

Although the hospital did not carry out the burial itself, Lumsden acknowledged its responsibility for choosing the contractor and making the arrangements.

“We should have known exactly how horses were handled after leaving our hospital, and we did not. That process is not good enough, and it must change.”

REC said it had stopped using the service provider and was reviewing how its contractors handle horses after death, from collection through to burial. It is also working with the facility operators and the state’s horse racing authority to establish what happened.

“No owner should have to go through what So You Are’s connections have experienced this week. We will make sure it does not happen again. Out of respect for So You Are and those who loved him, we ask that the footage not be shared further.”

#11042. SpaceX wants to build AI data centers in space. Will it work?

Imagine if one company could become the railroad, electric utility and cloud-computing provider of the emerging space economy. That potential fueled excitement around the long-anticipated initial public offering of SpaceX. Investors are not simply betting on rockets anymore. They are betting on an entire orbital ecosystem.

Among the most ambitious and challenging ideas riding this wave of enthusiasm is something that sounds almost like science fiction: orbital data centers. SpaceX may be one of the most well-known companies seeking to build them, but it is not the only one.

The logic is seductive: Launch the data centers into orbit, where solar energy is abundant and land, water and local power grids are no longer constraints. As artificial intelligence drives an explosion in computing demand, companies are pitching orbital data centers as a way to escape the growing environmental and infrastructure pressures of Earth-based computing. Data centers often also face backlash from the public at having these centers located in their communities.

But there is a vast difference between launching satellites and operating an industrial-scale computing infrastructure in orbit. Space is unforgiving. Radiation damages electronics. The electronics generate enormous amounts of heat, and getting rid of that heat is surprisingly difficult in space. Repairs are extraordinarily expensive, and every pound launched into orbit still carries a significant cost.

We are engineering professors who study data center design and space systems engineering. Building a space-based data center will involve considerations from both sides.

First off, consider what goes into an Earth-based data center, like those that you’ve probably begun to see pop up everywhere. These facilities power cloud computing, video streaming, online banking, scientific computing and, increasingly, artificial intelligence. But a data center is much more than a room full of servers.

A data center needs several things to operate reliably. The first is electric power. Servers, networking equipment and storage devices consume large amounts of electricity, and that power demand is growing rapidly with AI.

The second is cooling. Almost all the electricity consumed by servers eventually becomes heat. If that heat is not removed quickly and reliably, equipment performance drops, failures increase and the data center can shut down. Cooling systems often include air handling units, chillers, cooling towers, pumps and, increasingly, liquid-cooling equipment. In many facilities, cooling is the largest energy consumer after the computing equipment itself.

The third is physical infrastructure, including the necessary land, buildings, structural support, backup power, water systems, communication networks and maintenance access. Data centers also need to be close enough to users and network backbones to provide fast digital services.

In short, Earth-based data centers are large electrical and thermal infrastructure systems built around computing hardware.

So what would it take to build these data centers in space, and why are companies finding this possibility such an interesting business proposition?

As on Earth, these data centers would require massive amounts of power. In space, this power would come from solar panels. The Sun always shines in space and can’t be blocked by clouds. However, depending on the orbit the solar panels are put in, the Earth may shadow them for some portion of the orbit.

And even the best solar cells available today can convert only about half the sunlight that hits them to electricity.

Another potential advantage found in space is cooling. The cold background of space (near minus 455 degrees Fahrenheit, or minus 270 degrees Celsius) creates an opportunity: waste heat from the data center could escape into space through radiators, keeping the electronics cool.

In principle, that design could eliminate some of the bulky and water-intensive cooling infrastructure used on Earth. However, those thermal radiators would require a large amount of surface area, and that would be in addition to the area required by the solar panels.

In space, there is no air to blow across hot equipment and help heat escape. The heat has to leave as infrared radiation, which is a relatively slow process. As a result, removing 10 megawatts of waste heat can require radiator surfaces comparable to the size of two football fields.

Space-based data centers could also avoid some of the local conflicts that come with building large data centers on the ground. Many communities resist new data center developments because of their land use, energy and water demand, and noise and environmental impact.

A space-based system would avoid competing for local land and water resources, and it would not generate neighborhood noise or require local zoning approval in the same way.

However, space is already getting crowded, and launching thousands of large orbital data centers would accelerate this issue. Orbital debris and micrometeorites are hazards because they can puncture the space data center, and a worst-case collision could destroy it and create even more space debris.

The frequency of space launches necessary to send all the equipment to orbit may also become a concern for some communities. SpaceX has had protests at its launch complex in Boca Chica, Texas, from local activists who argue that its rocket testing and launches damage the surrounding environment.

All that data would need to be sent between Earth and these data centers – and between the data centers themselves – using radio waves or laser communications systems. Although satellite constellations such as Starlink and Amazon Leo have demonstrated that doing this is possible, the amount of data sent to and from space would balloon.

These data centers, along with their solar panels and radiators, cannot be launched in one piece and would need to be assembled in space. This process would require new equipment for in-space servicing, assembly and manufacturing.

Another key challenge is the refresh cycle of computing hardware. Data center servers are not built to last forever. Operators on Earth usually replace or upgrade hardware every three to five years as chips improve, workloads change and equipment ages.

And equipment failures can require replacing components. The refresh and repair processes are relatively straightforward on Earth, where workers can physically remove and replace servers.

In space, refresh and repair becomes much harder. Hardware sent to orbit may be difficult or too expensive to upgrade. If the computing platform cannot be updated, or too many components fail, it may become obsolete long before the surrounding infrastructure reaches the end of its useful life.

In a field where performance improves so rapidly and demand from computing continues to increase, this hurdle could prove a major economic and operational challenge.

Then there is the harshness of space. These data centers would be in a near vacuum, with constant radiation hitting them. And depending on their orbit, they would go from hot when in the sunlight to cold in Earth’s shadow many times a day. All of these challenges, and more, are issues that will need to be addressed.

Despite these challenges, companies are moving forward with designing space-based data centers. SpaceX just announced the design for its AI1 Compute Satellite, which it hopes to use as an orbital data center spacecraft. However, this satellite is 100 to 1,000 times less capable than current Earth-based data centers.

Not every computing task makes sense to do in space. Many data center applications depend on fast response times and close connections to users on Earth. Financial transactions, interactive AI services and most cloud applications are extremely sensitive to delay.

More feasible early applications may be those that are less latency-sensitive and more tightly connected to space operations. Examples could include processing Earth observation data from satellites, military or intelligence data processing, scientific computing related to space missions, or specialized computing for satellites and other space assets.

In other words, the first viable space data centers may serve space-based customers before they compete with mainstream cloud data centers on Earth.

#11039. How to Get a YouTube API Key and Use the Data API (2026)

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

You need a YouTube API key the moment your app has to read anything from YouTube: a video's length, its view count, a channel's latest uploads. Getting one takes about five minutes. Using it well takes a little longer, because YouTube changed how its quota works in June 2026, and plenty of guides still describe the old rules.

In this guide we'll get a key, lock it down and test it. Then we'll use it from Node.js for three real jobs: adding up the length of a playlist, listing a channel's videos without spending a search, and estimating what a video earned. Along the way we'll cover what the YouTube API costs (nothing), how the 2026 quota works, and the errors you're most likely to hit.

A YouTube API key identifies your project to the YouTube Data API v3. With just a key, you can read public data:

A key isn't enough for anything that touches someone's account. Uploading videos, editing playlists or reading private data needs OAuth 2.0, where the user signs in and grants your app access.

You'll need a Google account. The YouTube Data API is free, so there's nothing to pay for.

The restrictions matter more than they look. A key is just a string in a URL, and anyone who copies an unrestricted one can spend your daily quota for you. With an API restriction, the key is useless for other Google APIs. With an application restriction, it only works from your server or your site. Changes to restrictions can take up to five minutes to apply, so don't panic if a request fails straight after you save.

Before writing any code, check the key with a single request. This one asks for the first video ever uploaded to YouTube, and uses the fields parameter to trim the response down to four values:

That request cost 1 unit of your daily quota, which brings us to the question everyone asks.

Yes. There's no charge per request and no paid plan. Instead you get a daily quota, and since June 1, 2026 it's split into separate buckets:

Google's quota calculator lists the cost of every method. Quotas reset at midnight Pacific Time. Every request costs at least 1 unit, including requests that fail, so a retry loop around a broken call can use up a day's quota on its own.

If you've read older guides, search is the big change. A search used to cost 100 units out of the same 10,000, so 100 searches emptied your whole day. Uploads were worse still, at about 1,600 units each until December 2025. Now both have their own buckets, and the 10,000 units are left for everything else.

To give you a feel for what 10,000 units buys, here's what each example in this guide costs:

You can't pay for more. To go above the defaults, fill in the YouTube API Services Audit and Quota Extension Form, which is linked from Google's page on quota and compliance audits. YouTube first audits your project to check it follows the YouTube API Services Terms of Service, then someone from the API team gets back to you. Google doesn't give a timeline, so apply well before you need the extra quota.

Most projects never need to. The rest of this guide is about getting a lot done with very few units.

The examples need Node.js 20.6 or later, for the built-in fetch and the --env-file flag. There are no packages to install. Make a folder with a .env file in it:

Every example imports one small helper. It builds the URL, adds your key and turns API errors into readable messages:

Run any example with node --env-file=.env followed by the file name.

YouTube doesn't tell you how long a playlist is. To work it out, you need two methods:

Durations come back in ISO 8601 format. PT15M33S is 15 minutes 33 seconds, PT1H2M3S adds an hour, and a stream longer than a day looks like P1DT2H3M4S. The toSeconds() function below handles all three.

Here it is on freeCodeCamp's Full Courses in One Video playlist:

That's 184 hours of courses for 4 units of quota. Private and deleted videos still show up in a playlist's items, but videos.list doesn't return them, which is why the script prints "52 of 52 videos available". On a playlist with removed videos, the two numbers will differ.

This is the core of the free YouTube playlist length calculator I built on createthat.ai. The live version adds a target time box that works out the speed you need to finish by a deadline, and it accepts channel, video and Shorts links as well as playlists.

The obvious way to find a channel's videos is to search for them. Don't: search is now capped at 100 calls a day, and it isn't built to list everything a channel has posted. Every channel has an uploads playlist instead, and you can reach it in two cheap calls:

Two units, and none of your 100 daily searches. To get every upload rather than the newest five, page through the playlist with the same pageToken loop as Example 1.

videos.list with part=statistics returns a video's view, like and comment counts. Dislike counts have been private since December 2021 and favoriteCount is always 0, so don't build anything on those. The script also copes with a missing like or comment count, which you'll see on some videos.

This time the fields parameter trims the response to the values we use. That makes the response smaller, but it doesn't change the cost: the call is 1 unit either way.

Here it is on freeCodeCamp's Learn Python full course, one of the most-watched videos in that playlist:

Treat the last line as a rough range, not an answer. The Data API doesn't share anyone's revenue, so any estimate is views multiplied by an RPM: what a creator keeps per 1,000 views. RPM moves a lot with the topic and where the viewers live, and the estimate assumes the video shows ads, which not every channel does.

And sometimes you don't need an API at all. When I built a font tester, I needed a list of Google Fonts. The Google Fonts Developer API would have meant a key and a call on every visit, for a list that barely changes. Instead I generated a file of the 500 most popular fonts once, from Google's public font metadata, and the page loads it like any other file. No key, no quota and nothing to fail.

When a call fails, the response's error.message and error.details[0].reason tell you why. These are the ones you'll see most:

For the invalid key error, the full response looks like this:

Yes. Creating a key and calling the YouTube Data API costs nothing. You're limited by the daily quota instead, and there's no paid tier: more quota comes through an audit.

In the Google Cloud console, select your project and go to APIs & Services > Credentials. Your keys are listed under API keys, with a Show key option next to each one.

It works, but anyone can read the key from your page. If you do it, restrict the key to your website and to the YouTube Data API v3. A small server route that calls the API for you is safer, and it gives you somewhere to cache results.

By default, 100 searches, 100 uploads and 10,000 units for everything else. Most reads cost 1 unit and videos.list takes 50 IDs per call, so that's enough for the details of up to 500,000 videos a day.

Getting a YouTube API key is the easy part: one project, one API to enable, one key and two restrictions. The 2026 quota is generous if you work with it:

All three examples together cost 7 units, out of 10,000. From here, try commentThreads.list to pull a video's comments 100 at a time, or extend Example 2 to page through a channel's entire upload history and add it all up with the code from Example 1.

#11037. Millions of exploding stars could soon reveal dark energy's secrets

Researchers led by the Institute of Cosmos Sciences of the University of Barcelona (ICCUB) have developed a new technique that could greatly improve how scientists study the expansion of the Universe and investigate the mysterious force known as dark energy.

Published in Nature Astronomy, the research introduces a framework called CIGaRS that can extract far more information from Type Ia supernovae, the powerful stellar explosions used to measure vast cosmic distances. Unlike many current approaches, the method relies mainly on imaging data rather than expensive spectroscopic observations. The advance is expected to help astronomers take full advantage of the enormous datasets that will soon arrive from next generation sky surveys, particularly those conducted by the Vera C. Rubin Observatory.

Type Ia supernovae occur when white dwarf stars explode. Because these explosions reach nearly the same intrinsic brightness, astronomers use them as "standard candles": by comparing their actual brightness with how bright they appear from Earth, researchers can calculate their distance.

These measurements played a crucial role in the discovery that the Universe is expanding at an accelerating rate. Scientists attribute that acceleration to dark energy, one of the most significant unsolved questions in modern physics.

However, there is an important complication. Type Ia supernovae are not perfectly identical.

How Host Galaxies Affect Supernova Measurements

Over the past 20 years, astronomers have found that a supernova's observed brightness is influenced by the galaxy where it occurs. Supernovae found in older or more massive galaxies can appear slightly different from those occurring in younger or less massive galaxies.

Researchers have typically accounted for these differences using relatively simple correction methods. While useful, these approximations can limit the accuracy of distance measurements and, in turn, the precision of cosmological studies.

A Unified Model of Supernovae and the Universe

The new framework addresses this challenge by modeling multiple factors simultaneously. Rather than treating each component independently, the researchers built a single, integrated model that includes the supernova explosions themselves, their host galaxies, the dust that alters their light, changes in supernova rates throughout cosmic history, and even the expansion of the Universe.

By connecting all of these ingredients within one statistical and physical framework, the team can capture relationships that are often overlooked when the pieces are analyzed separately.

"A powerful way of modelling the Universe is to simulate it ab initio in the computer using Bayesian inference," says Raúl Jiménez (ICREA-ICCUB), co-author of the study. "This provides a way to vary all possible parameters at the same time to predict what Universe we live in. Furthermore, by having this capacity, one can look into possible 'unknown unknown' systematics to understand their effect. The impact of these systematics in our inference is arguably the most important missing ingredient in current approaches to model the Universe."

Using Artificial Intelligence To Analyze the Cosmos

Building such a comprehensive model would normally require enormous computing power. To make the approach practical, the researchers turned to a modern technique called simulation-based inference.

The process begins with scientists generating large numbers of simulated universes based on physical models. A neural network (a type of artificial intelligence) then learns how the simulated observations relate to the physical properties that produced them. Once trained, the system can compare real astronomical observations with its simulations and determine the most likely underlying parameters.

This strategy makes it possible to analyze tens of thousands of supernovae simultaneously, a task that would be impractical using traditional techniques.

Accurate Galaxy Distances From Images Alone

One of the study's most significant findings is that the framework can determine galaxy distances (redshifts) with high accuracy using only imaging data.

Redshift measures how much a galaxy's light has been stretched as the Universe expands. It provides information about both the galaxy's distance and how far back in time we are observing it.

According to the researchers, the new method delivers redshift estimates with precision comparable to spectroscopic measurements, but without requiring spectra. This capability is especially important because upcoming surveys are expected to identify millions of supernova candidates, while only a small percentage can realistically receive spectroscopic follow-up observations.

Ready for the Rubin Observatory Data Deluge

The Vera C. Rubin Observatory, currently being built in Chile, is expected to begin a decade long survey of the sky in the near future. During that mission, it will discover an unprecedented number of supernovae. Roughly 99% of those objects will be observed only photometrically, meaning through images taken in different colors rather than detailed spectra.

The CIGaRS framework was specifically developed with this challenge in mind.

"Unlike other frameworks, which require analytic simplifications, our no-compromise end-to-end simulation-based inference approach is uniquely capable of extracting the full cosmological and astrophysical information from the Rubin Observatory's hard-earned data, while avoiding the pitfalls of selection and modelling biases," says Konstantin Karchev (ICCUB-SISSA Trieste), lead author of the study.

The benefits extend beyond measuring dark energy. The framework also provides new information about the origins of Type Ia supernovae themselves.

By reconstructing how supernova occurrence rates vary with the ages of stars in different galaxies, the model helps scientists investigate long standing questions about the systems that eventually produce these explosions.

The researchers found that combining physics based simulations with artificial intelligence can overcome several limitations of current cosmological methods. They estimate that the approach could improve cosmological constraints by as much as a factor of four compared with traditional techniques that depend only on a relatively small sample of spectroscopically observed supernovae.

As the Rubin Observatory prepares to usher in a new era of astronomical discovery, tools such as CIGaRS could help scientists extract the maximum amount of information from its observations and gain a deeper understanding of the Universe.
